  # ROLE                                                                        
  You are a senior AI engineer (15+ years in software, 4+ building production   
  LLM agent systems                                                             
  with retrieval and memory layers) who also teaches Gen AI to undergraduate    
  students. You                                                                 
  review training material with two hats on at once: the practitioner who       
  checks whether the                                                            
  technical content is correct and current, and the instructor who checks       
  whether a 2nd-year                                                            
  B.Tech student can actually walk out of the session able to build the thing.  
                                                                                
  # DOCUMENT UNDER REVIEW                                                       
  - Type: TR Doc (training resource for a live/recorded session)                
  - Session: Building Agents with Memory                                        
  - Sub-topic under review: Long-term Memory                                    
  - Audience: 2nd-year B.Tech students. Assume they ALREADY know: what Gen AI   
  and LLMs are,                                                                 
    n8n (visual workflow building), building basic LLM applications,            
  tool/function calling,                                                        
    and the concept of agents.                                                  
  - Assume they DO NOT reliably know: embeddings and vector spaces, vector      
  databases,                                                                    
    similarity search, chunking, database schema design, async/queue patterns,  
  evaluation                                                                    
    methodology, or production cost/latency reasoning.                          
  - Purpose: teach one sub-topic well enough that students can implement it in  
  a hands-on lab.                                                               
  - Document follows the ---DOC--- marker.                                      
                                                                                
  # WHAT I WANT                                                                 
  A rigorous but constructive review that makes this document (a) technically   
  accurate and                                                                  
  current, and (b) genuinely teachable to the audience above. Every criticism   
  must come with a                                                              
  concrete fix. Do not rewrite the whole document.                              
                                                                                
  # HOW TO REVIEW — PASSES IN ORDER                                             
  Pass 1 — Student comprehension: Read once as a 2nd-year student with exactly  
  the prior                                                                     
  knowledge listed. Mark every point where you'd stall, re-read, or need to     
  search elsewhere.                                                             
  Record these before critiquing as an expert.                                  
  Pass 2 — Technical audit: Verify claims, terminology, code, diagrams,         
  tool/framework                                                                
  references and version currency. Check internal consistency across sections.  
  Pass 3 — Pedagogy: Does the sequence build from what they know (agents, tool  
  calling) to                                                                   
  what's new (persistent memory)? Is each concept motivated by a problem before 
  it's named?                                                                   
  Pass 4 — Lab readiness: Could a student actually build this from the doc? Is  
  there a runnable                                                              
  path end to end?                                                              
  Pass 5 — Language & presentation: jargon load, sentence length,               
  figure/caption quality,                                                       
  formatting, attribution.                                                      
                                                                                
  # TOPIC-SPECIFIC CONTENT CHECKLIST                                            
  Mark each as Covered well / Covered weakly / Missing / Wrong, and say whether 
  it belongs at                                                                 
  this level or should be explicitly deferred.                                  
                                                                                
  Conceptual foundation                                                         
  - The motivating failure: a concrete before/after showing an agent that       
  forgets, and why                                                              
    that breaks the user experience.                                            
  - Short-term (context window / conversation buffer) vs long-term (persisted   
  across sessions)                                                              
    — is the boundary drawn crisply, with a decision rule for which to use?     
  - The distinction between long-term memory and RAG over a document corpus.    
  This is the single                                                            
    most commonly muddled point — check it explicitly.                          
  - Memory categories, if introduced: semantic (facts about the user/world),    
  episodic (past                                                                
    interactions/events), procedural (learned how-to or instructions). Are      
  these introduced with                                                         
    examples, or just as a taxonomy to memorise?                                
                                                                                
  Mechanics                                                                     
  - Write path: what triggers a memory write, what gets extracted vs stored     
  raw, who decides                                                              
    (LLM-as-extractor vs explicit tool call vs rules).                          
  - Storage: what actually sits in the database — schema, metadata, namespacing 
  per user.                                                                     
  - Retrieval path: query formulation, semantic search vs keyword vs hybrid,    
  top-k, metadata                                                               
    filtering, recency weighting, relevance thresholds.                         
  - Injection: how retrieved memories enter the prompt, where in the prompt,    
  and the token                                                                 
    budget tradeoff.                                                            
  - Maintenance: deduplication, conflict resolution when a new fact contradicts 
  an old one,                                                                   
    updates vs appends, decay/forgetting, summarisation/consolidation.          
  - Embeddings and vector search: given the audience, is there enough intuition 
  (not maths) to                                                                
    make retrieval non-magical? Or is it hand-waved?                            
                                                                                
  Practice                                                                      
  - Tool/function-calling framing: is long-term memory presented as save_memory 
  / search_memory                                                               
    tools the agent invokes? This connects directly to prior knowledge and      
  should be exploited.                                                          
  - n8n grounding: the audience knows n8n, so is there at least one worked      
  example in that                                                               
    vocabulary (memory node, vector store node, workflow shape) before or       
  alongside any code?                                                           
  - Tooling named: is it current and are choices justified rather than listed?  
  (e.g. vector                                                                  
    stores, LangGraph/LangChain memory, Mem0, Zep, Letta, plain                 
  Postgres+pgvector.) Flag                                                      
    anything deprecated, renamed, or version-sensitive.                         
  - Working code or workflow that runs, with imports, environment setup and     
  expected output.                                                              
  - Failure modes and debugging: retrieving irrelevant memories, stale facts,   
  memory pollution,                                                             
    prompt bloat, latency.                                                      
  - Cost, latency and scale reasoning at a beginner-appropriate level.          
  - Privacy and safety: PII in memory, consent, deletion/right-to-forget,       
  multi-user isolation,                                                         
    prompt injection via poisoned memory. Flag if absent — it usually is.       
  - Evaluation: how do you know the memory layer is working? Any measurable     
  notion at all.                                                                
                                                                                
  # EVALUATION DIMENSIONS (score 1–5 with a one-line justification)             
  1. Technical accuracy and currency                                            
  2. Conceptual completeness (per checklist above)                              
  3. Prerequisite calibration — does it correctly assume what they know and     
  correctly teach                                                               
     what they don't? Flag both over-explaining known material and              
  under-explaining new material.                                                
  4. Logical scaffolding — problem before solution, concrete before abstract    
  5. Clarity and jargon control for a 2nd-year reader                           
  6. Hands-on implementability                                                  
  7. Learning aids — diagrams, worked examples, analogies, summaries,           
  self-checks                                                                   
  8. Presentation, formatting and attribution                                   
                                                                                
  # OUTPUT FORMAT                                                               
  ## 1. Verdict                                                                 
  3–4 sentences: overall quality, readiness (Reject / Major revision / Minor    
  revision / Ready),                                                            
  and the single highest-leverage change.                                       
                                                                                
  ## 2. Scorecard                                                               
  Table: Dimension | Score /5 | Justification. Plus an overall score.           
                                                                                
  ## 3. Content coverage matrix                                                 
  The topic-specific checklist as a table: Item | Status | Comment | Belongs at 
  this level?                                                                   
                                                                                
  ## 4. Strengths                                                               
  3–5 specific things to preserve, with locations. Concrete, not flattering.    
                                                                                
  ## 5. Findings                                                                
  Table: # | Location | Issue | Severity (Critical / Major / Minor / Nitpick) | 
  Impact on a                                                                   
  2nd-year student | Recommended fix. Ordered by severity. Critical = wrong, or 
  blocks                                                                        
  comprehension, or breaks the lab.                                             
                                                                                
  ## 6. Student-stall points                                                    
  Pass 1 output: exact places a target student loses the thread, and what to    
  add, define or                                                                
  reorder to prevent it. Include any term used before it is defined.            
                                                                                
  ## 7. Rewrite examples                                                        
  The 3 weakest passages: Original → Revised → what changed and why. Preserve   
  the author's voice.                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 8. Additions worth making                                                  
  Be concrete and specific to this topic. For example: describe the             
  write-path/read-path diagram                                                  
  you'd draw, sketch the one worked example you'd add, propose an analogy for   
  embeddings that                                                               
  doesn't break, draft 4–5 self-check questions, suggest one debugging          
  exercise.                                                                     
                                                                                
  ## 9. Suggested session flow                                                  
  If the ordering is off, propose a revised outline with rough time allocation  
  for a                                                                         
  <INSERT DURATION>-minute slot, marking what to cut if time runs short.        
                                                                                
  ## 10. Prioritised action list                                                
  Numbered and sequenced, each tagged quick / medium / substantial. Max 10      
  items.                                                                        
                                                                                
  ## 11. Questions for the author                                               
  Anything you could not judge without more information.                        
                                                                                
  # RULES                                                                       
  - Evidence over impression: cite the section or line for every criticism.     
  Keep quotes short.                                                            
  - Never invent facts, APIs, benchmarks or citations. Mark anything you cannot 
  verify as                                                                     
    "UNVERIFIED — author to confirm" rather than guessing.                      
  - Flag any API, library name or feature that may have changed, and say what   
  to re-check.                                                                  
  - Separate "this is wrong" from "this is my preference" and label which you   
  mean.                                                                         
  - Judge against a 2nd-year B.Tech reader with the stated prerequisites — not  
  against a                                                                     
    senior engineer, and not against a total beginner.                          
  - Do not inflate scores to be encouraging, and do not soften Critical         
  findings.

● Audit complete — I ran the server, the Inspector, both transports, and the SDK
  internals. Writing it up.

  One note on the brief: it says "Sub-topic under review: Long-term Memory" with
  a memory-specific checklist, but the document is Build Your Own MCP Server.
  I've reviewed the actual document and adapted §3's checklist to MCP authoring,
  keeping your output structure.

  ---

  1. Verdict

  This is a well-structured, genuinely well-motivated document with an unusually
  strong conceptual spine — the primitive-ownership table and the "when should
  you build a server" decision path are better than most MCP material in
  circulation, and the SDK 2.x API claims are all correct, which I verified by
  running the code. But four things are wrong in ways a student will hit: the
  doc's central Step 6 lesson is contradicted by its own final code, the
  headline print() warning is no longer true for the SDK version the doc
  mandates, the resources/read expected output omits the protocol envelope, and
  save_learner_profile takes user_id from the model — undoing the isolation
  lesson from Session 33. Readiness: Major revision — the structure and most
  content are keepers; the specific claims need fixing. Highest-leverage change:
  make the three tools wrap their HTTP calls in try/except → ToolError, and 
  tell students that unhandled exceptions are stripped of detail — because that
  single fix makes Step 6 true, makes the final code correct, and turns the
  doc's best idea into something that actually works.

  ---

  2. Scorecard

  Dimension: 1. Technical accuracy and currency
  Score /5: 3
  Justification: Every API claim verified correct against mcp 2.1.1 (MCPServer,
  the exceptions path, transports, httpx2 as a real dep, Inspector's node
  ≥22.19.0, -32602). But four verified-wrong claims: the print() rule, the
  resources/read output, the structured_content example, and the Step 6 error
  behaviour as implemented.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 2. Conceptual completeness
  Score /5: 3
  Justification: Tools, resources, transports, errors, versioning, and the
  build-or-not decision are all covered well. Prompts are promised and never
  built; async, auth, and multi-user isolation are absent rather than deferred.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 3. Prerequisite calibration
  Score /5: 4
  Justification: Exploits tool-calling and Session 43 well — "the same lesson as

  the @tool decorator, except now published over a protocol" is exactly the
  right bridge. Loses a point for leaning on uv, JSON-RPC, and stderr-vs-stdout
   without introduction.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 4. Logical scaffolding
  Score /5: 5
  Justification: The arc table (L26–32), then "what is running at the other end
  of that URL?", then a minimal server before the real one, then transports,
  then errors. Problem precedes solution throughout.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 5. Clarity and jargon control
  Score /5: 4
  Justification: Short sentences, strong one-liners. "A tool is something the
  model decides to do. A resource is something the application decides to show
  it." Held back by undefined JSON-RPC, subprocess, semver, DNS-rebinding.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 6. Hands-on implementability
  Score /5: 3
  Justification: The server, both transports, and all three Inspector modes
  genuinely work — I ran them. But uv vs pip diverges from the companion code,
  one expected output is wrong, and the showcase failure example is flaky.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 7. Learning aids
  Score /5: 3
  Justification: Three good, accurate diagrams and ten well-built tables. Zero
  self-check questions, no exercise, no debugging section.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 8. Presentation and attribution
  Score /5: 3
  Justification: Clean and consistent. But "From the official documentation:"
  quotes a claim that is now false for this SDK, with no URL — the worst
  combination of unattributed and outdated.

  Overall: 3.5 / 5

  ---

  ---

  4. Strengths

  1. L60–78, the primitives table with the ownership column. Leading with who 
     controls it — Model / Application / User — and then stating the trap
     outright: "A tool is something the model decides to do. A resource is
     something the application decides to show it." Then it applies the rule to
     its own two examples. This is the single most-muddled point in MCP and the
     doc handles it in under twenty lines.
  2. L36–52, "Heads Up: We Are Leaving Colab." It explains why rather than
     announcing a constraint: stdio means the client launches your server as a
     subprocess, and "a Colab notebook is not a command another program on your
     computer can launch." Every course that moves students off a notebook
     should do this.
  3. L600–650, the four-way comparison and decision path. Built-in tool → custom
     @tool → skill → MCP server, with a cost column and four ordered questions.
     The closing warning — "An MCP server is not just a function — it is a
     deployable artifact with a public contract… Build the @tool first" — is the
     judgement most tutorials never teach. Preserve verbatim.
  4. L490–520, schemas-as-public-contract. The semver table tied to
     client-visible consequences ("Make an argument required → Major, existing
     calls become invalid") turns versioning from bureaucracy into a consequence
     students can reason about. "Once someone else connects to your server,
     changing an argument name is not a refactor — it is a breaking API change."
  5. The three diagrams are accurate. I checked all three against runtime
     behaviour. mcp-error-paths.png's -32602 claim for a missing resource is
     correct — I captured MCPError code=-32602. mcp-host-client-server.png's
     "one client per server" matches Session 43's vocabulary. This is rare; most
     diagrams in review drift from their text.

  ---

  5. Findings

  #: 1
  Location: Step 6 (L332–360) + final code
  Issue: The doc's central lesson is contradicted by its own code. Step 6
  teaches
  "raise ToolError with a descriptive message so the model can act on it." But
  all three tools leave the httpx2 call unguarded, so the most common real
  failure — a network blip or timeout — is not a ToolError. Verified: an
  unguarded failure returns is_error=True, text='Error  executing tool 
  network_fail' — no detail. Wrapped in ToolError, the same failure returns the
   full message. The SDK masks unhandled exceptions (UnexpectedToolError), and
  the doc never says so. I hit this live: one run of the author's own test
  printed 'Error executing tool search_jobs' with the message stripped.
  Severity: Critical
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: The student writes the tools as shown, believes
  they've built good error handling, and ships a server whose real-world
  failures tell the model nothing. The lesson they were just taught is silently
   false in their own code.
  Recommended fix: Two parts. (a) Wrap the HTTP call in every tool: try: r = 
  httpx2.get(...) except Exception as e: raise ToolError(f"Could  not reach the
   jobs API: {type(e).__name__}"). (b) Add the missing rule to Step 6: *"The
  SDK only forwards the message when you raise ToolError. Any other exception
  is masked to Error executing tool <name> — the model gets the name and
  nothing else. An unhandled exception is a silent error."* This is a better
  lesson than the one currently there.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Location: L145–170, "Never print() in a stdio server"
  Issue: The claim is false for the SDK this doc mandates. mcp 2.x deliberately
  protects against it: mcp/server/stdio.py takes a private duplicate of fd 1
  for the wire and calls _open_stdout_diversion() → os.dup(2), re-pointing
  stdout at stderr. Verified: I put print() inside a tool and called it through
   the Inspector — "isError": false, worked fine. I also put a print() at
  import time, before mcp.run() — also fine. Yet the doc calls this "the single
   most common beginner mistake" and quotes it as a hard rule from "the
  official documentation" with no URL.
  Severity: Critical (wrong, and prominently placed)
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: A student who tests the claim finds it false,
  which undermines every other warning in the document. Worse, the
  quoted-as-authority framing teaches them not to check.
  Recommended fix: Keep the advice, replace the justification: "On stdio, the 
  JSON-RPC messages travel over stdout — so anything else written there would 
  corrupt them. SDK 2.x guards against this: it takes a private copy of stdout 
  for the protocol and redirects your program's stdout to stderr, so a stray 
  print() no longer breaks the connection. Don't rely on that. Other SDKs and 
  older versions have no such guard, and logging is what you want anyway — it 
  goes to stderr, carries levels and timestamps, and won't vanish when you 
  switch transports." Add the URL, or drop the quotation marks. UNVERIFIED — 
  author to  confirm which docs page the quote came from; it reads like 1.x-era
   text.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Location: Step 7 (L410–425)
  Issue: The resources/read expected output is wrong. The doc shows a bare
  {"name": "Anil", ...}. Verified actual output:
  {"contents":[{"uri":"learner://profile/learner_001","mimeType":"application/js
  on","text":"{\n
    \"name\": \"Anil\", ...}"}]} — an envelope, with the payload as an escaped
  string.
  Severity: Critical (breaks the lab step)
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: The student runs the exact command, sees a
  different shape, and can't tell whether their resource is broken. And they
  miss the most instructive output in the session.
  Recommended fix: Show the real envelope, then use it: "Notice you get an 
  envelope, not your dictionary. contents is an array because one URI may 
  return several pieces; mimeType is the one we set; and your JSON arrives as a
   string inside text — the protocol carries text, so the client parses it. 
  This is what mime_type was for." This turns a wrong output into the clearest
  possible demonstration of what a resource read actually is.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Location: Step 3, save_learner_profile + Step 4 resource
  Issue: user_id comes from the model. It is a normal tool argument, so any
  client can write any user_id, and learner://profile/{user_id} will read any
  profile back. In Session 33 the same agent got user_id from runtime.context
  where the model could never see or set it — and that doc calls a shared
  user_id a privacy bug. This doc silently reverses the lesson.
  Severity: Critical (wrong, and contradicts a prior session)
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: The student learns to make identity a
  model-supplied parameter, one session after being taught that identity must
  never be.
  Recommended fix: Don't remove it — name it. Add a warning box: "Notice what we
   
  just did: user_id is an argument the model fills in. That is fine for a local
   single-user server, and wrong for a shared one — any client could read any 
  learner's profile by guessing an id. In Session 33 the user_id came from 
  runtime.context, where the model could not see it. Over stdio your OS process
   is the boundary. Over HTTP it is not, which is what the authentication note 
  in Step 5 is really about." This connects transport, auth and identity in one
   paragraph and costs nothing.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Location: Step 6, "Structured Output — For Free"
  Issue: The worked example shows the wrong tool. The section is about
  search_jobs -> list[dict] and displays its array output schema, then shows
  structured_content: {'result': 'Saved profile for Anil.'} — which is
  save_learner_profile's string result. Verified: that value is correct for 
  save_learner_profile; it cannot come from the schema shown.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: The student tries to reconcile an array schema
  with a string result and concludes they've misunderstood structured output.
  Recommended fix: Show search_jobs's actual structured content — an array of
  job
  dicts — under its own schema. If you want the string example too, label it as
   the other tool.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Location: Step 6, "What This Looks Like in Practice"
  Issue: The showcase failure is flaky. It depends on JSearch returning zero
  results for "Underwater Basket Weaving in Atlantis". Verified: usually 0 (4/4
   in one burst), but I also got a run returning 1 job, which makes the tool
  succeed — isError=False.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: The student's demonstration of error handling
  may
  quietly demonstrate success instead, with no explanation available.
  Recommended fix: Use a failure that cannot vary. Unset the key: "Rename 
  RAPID_API_KEY in your .env and call the tool — you get the ToolError you 
  wrote, deterministically." That also exercises the guard clause the tools
  already have, and works offline.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Location: L66 primitives table
  Issue: Prompts are promised and never delivered. Introduced as one of three
  primitives with an ownership row, then never built, never mentioned, never
  deferred.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: The student finishes believing they've seen all
  three primitives and cannot write the one they didn't see.
  Recommended fix: Either add ~15 lines (@mcp.prompt() returning a templated
  string, plus one line on how a user invokes it), or say so plainly: "We build
   tools and resources today. Prompts are the  third primitive — user-invoked 
  templates — and we leave them  for you to explore." A named deferral is
  completely different from silence.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Location: Step 1 vs mcp_code/README.md
  Issue: The doc says uv; the companion code uses python3 -m venv + pip. Also uv

  is not installed on this machine, so Step 1 is the one step I could not
  verify end-to-end. Everything downstream I ran with ./.venv/bin/python, which
   worked identically.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: A student following the doc and then opening the

  companion code finds two different setups and doesn't know which is
  authoritative.
  Recommended fix: Pick one. If uv (reasonable — it's what the official docs
  use), update the companion README and add the Windows install line, since
  curl … | sh is macOS/Linux only and the doc doesn't say so. Add one line: "If
   you already have a venv,  python server.py works anywhere this doc says uv 
  run  server.py."
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 9
  Location: All three tools
  Issue: Sync def doing 30-second network I/O. async def is never mentioned. The

  SDK supports it; a sync tool blocks the server for the duration.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: With one client it's invisible; the first time a

  student's HTTP server has two callers, it mysteriously serialises.
  Recommended fix: An explicit deferral plus one sentence of why: "Our tools are
   
  ordinary def functions, so each call blocks the server until it returns. 
  That's fine for one local client. A shared HTTP server should use async def 
  with an async HTTP client — the same pattern as the await client.get_tools() 
  you've already seen." The audience doesn't know async, so don't teach it —
  just name the limit.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 10
  Location: Whole doc
  Issue: No self-checks, no exercise, no debugging section. 716 lines, zero
  retention aids.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Nothing to test understanding against, and MCP
  failures are especially opaque (a stdio server that dies produces no output
  at all).
  Recommended fix: See §8 for five questions and a three-fault debugging
  exercise.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 11
  Location: Step 1
  Issue: uv add "mcp[cli]" but the mcp CLI is never used. The doc tests
  exclusively with npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector.
  Severity: Minor
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Harmless, but the student installs an extra and
  never learns why.
  Recommended fix: Either drop to uv add mcp, or add one line noting the [cli]
  extra provides the mcp command and that we use the Node Inspector instead
  because it also gives a browser UI and a TUI.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 12
  Location: L152
  Issue: "From the official documentation:" with no URL.
  Severity: Minor
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Unfalsifiable for the student, and in this case
  the quoted claim is outdated (Finding 2).
  Recommended fix: Cite the page, or state it in your own voice.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 13
  Location: mcp-error-paths.png
  Issue: The -32602 label is correct for the SDK (verified). Whether it matches
  the spec is a separate question — the spec defines a dedicated
  resource-not-found code.
  Severity: Nitpick
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: None — students see -32602.
  Recommended fix: Optionally add "(the Python SDK's code; the spec's own list
  differs)". UNVERIFIED — author to confirm the current spec's code before
  writing that parenthetical.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 14
  Location: Step 9
  Issue: "transport": "http" is not in langchain-mcp-adapters' type Literal
  (streamable_http), but the runtime dispatch accepts {"streamable_http", 
  "streamable-http", "http"} — verified in 0.3.2. So it works; a type checker
  will complain. Matches Session 43, so it's consistent.
  Severity: Nitpick (preference)
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: None.
  Recommended fix: Leave it. If you ever want type-clean code, "streamable_http"

  is the canonical spelling.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 15
  Location: skill_demand
  Issue: Tavily is called with api_key in the JSON body rather than an
  Authorization: Bearer header. Verified working — returned 200.
  Severity: Nitpick (currency watch)
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: None today.
  Recommended fix: No change needed; re-check if Tavily deprecates body auth.

  ---

  6. Student-stall points

  Read as a 2nd-year student with exactly the stated prerequisites.

  Where: L40, "launched as a subprocess"
  What stalls them: Subprocess is the load-bearing word in the whole "why not
  Colab" argument, and it's never defined. Students know tool calling, not
  process models.
  Fix: One clause: "launched as a subprocess — the client literally runs python 
  server.py the way you would in a terminal, and talks to it through that 
  program's input and output."
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: L128, "listening for JSON-RPC messages"
  What stalls them: JSON-RPC appears here for the first time and is used
  throughout (Step 6's whole table depends on it). Never defined.
  Fix: One line at first use: "JSON-RPC is just an agreed shape for 'call this 
  function with these arguments' sent as JSON. MCP is a set of these  messages 
  — tools/list, tools/call, resources/read — which is why the Inspector 
  commands you'll run later are named after them." This also pre-explains Step
  7's --method values.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: L152–165, stdout vs stderr
  What stalls them: The warning assumes students know a process has two output
  streams and that print and logging go to different ones. Nothing in the
  prerequisites covers this.
  Fix: Two sentences before the warning: "Every program has two output channels:
   
  stdout for its real output and stderr for messages about what it's doing. On 
  stdio, MCP uses stdout for the protocol — so stderr is where your own 
  messages belong."
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: Step 1 in full
  What stalls them: Four unfamiliar commands in a row (uv init, uv venv, source 
  .venv/bin/activate, uv add) with no statement of what each accomplishes, plus
   a macOS/Linux-only installer with no Windows path.
  Fix: A one-line comment per command, and a Windows line for uv. This is the
  first time the course leaves Colab, so this is exactly where a student gets
  stranded before writing any code.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: L322–326, Origin validation and DNS-rebinding
  What stalls them: Two security concepts named and neither explained, in a doc
  that otherwise explains everything. A student either ignores it or googles a
  rabbit hole.
  Fix: Either one plain sentence — "a malicious web page can otherwise trick 
  your
   browser into talking to servers on your own machine, so the server checks  
  who is asking" — or cut it to "add authentication before exposing it
  publicly" and defer.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: Step 8, "Minor — 1.1.0" / "Major — 2.0.0"
  What stalls them: The table is excellent but assumes semver. Students have
  used
  pip install versions; they haven't been taught what the three numbers mean.
  Fix: Half a line: "Version numbers read major.minor.patch — bumping the first 
  says 'this breaks existing callers'."
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: Step 6's two-error distinction
  What stalls them: This is the doc's hardest idea and it arrives as an abstract

  table before any concrete failure. Everywhere else the doc puts the concrete
  first.
  Fix: Invert it: show the failing call and its isError=True output first, then
  the table explaining why that path exists and what the other one is. Same
  content, matches the doc's own pattern.

  Terms used before definition: subprocess (L40), JSON-RPC (L128), stdout/stderr
  (L152), semver (Step 8), Origin header (L324), DNS-rebinding (L324), bearer 
  token (Step 5 table).

  ---

  7. Rewrite examples

  A. The print() warning — L145–170 (the Critical one)

  Original

  ▎ A stdio server talks to its client over stdout. Anything else written there 
  ▎ corrupts the message stream.
  ▎
  ▎ From the official documentation: "Writing to stdout will corrupt the 
  ▎ JSON-RPC messages and break your server."
  ▎
  ▎ print() writes to stdout, so keep it out of a stdio server entirely. […] 
  ▎ This is the single most common beginner mistake with MCP servers.

  Revised

  ▎ A stdio server talks to its client over stdout — the same channel print() 
  ▎ writes to. Anything else landing there would sit in the middle of the 
  ▎ protocol's messages and corrupt them.
  ▎
  ▎ SDK 2.x guards against this. When the server starts, it takes a private copy
  ▎ of stdout for the protocol and quietly redirects your program's stdout to 
  ▎ stderr — so a stray print() does not break the connection any more. Try it 
  ▎ if you like: it works.
  ▎
  ▎ Use logging anyway.
  ▎
  ▎ import logging
  ▎ logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)
  ▎
  ▎ logger.info("searching jobs")   # stderr — always safe
  ▎ print("searching jobs")         # works here, thanks to the SDK. Don't rely 
  ▎ on it.
  ▎
  ▎ Three reasons the rule outlives the guard: other MCP SDKs and older Python 
  ▎ versions have no such protection; logging gives you levels and timestamps 
  ▎ that print doesn't; and the moment you switch to HTTP your prints go to a 
  ▎ terminal nobody is reading. Keeping output on stderr is the habit — the SDK 
  ▎ just stopped punishing you for forgetting.

  What changed and why: the factual claim is now true for the mandated SDK
  (verified: mcp/server/stdio.py diverts fd 1 to fd 2, and a print() inside a
  tool returns "isError": false). The unattributed quote is gone. The advice
  survives with honest reasons, and inviting the student to test it builds the
  habit the doc wants rather than asking for trust.

  ---

  B. Step 6's failure example — the flaky, message-stripped one

  Original

  ▎ That is why our tools raise ToolError with a descriptive message. […]
  ▎
  ▎ Calling search_jobs with a skill that returns nothing:
  ▎ is_error=True
  ▎ content=[TextContent(type='text',
  ▎          text='Error executing tool search_jobs: No Underwater Basket 
  ▎ Weaving jobs found in Atlantis.')]

  Revised

  ▎ That is why our tools raise ToolError with a descriptive message. "No 
  ▎ Generative AI jobs found in Atlantis" is something a model can act on. A 
  ▎ generic crash is not.
  ▎
  ▎ Try it deterministically: comment out RAPID_API_KEY in your .env and call 
  ▎ the tool.
  ▎ is_error=True
  ▎ content=[TextContent(type='text',
  ▎          text='Error executing tool search_jobs: RAPID_API_KEY is not 
  ▎ configured on the server.')]
  ▎ The model reads that sentence and can tell the learner what went wrong.
  ▎
  ▎ Now the part most tutorials skip. The SDK only forwards your message when 
  ▎ you raise ToolError. Any other exception gets masked:
  ▎ is_error=True
  ▎ content=[TextContent(type='text', text='Error executing tool search_jobs')]
  ▎ That is all the model gets. No reason, no retry, nothing to explain to the 
  ▎ user.
  ▎
  ▎ Which means the try/except around our HTTP call is not defensive padding — 
  ▎ it is the difference between a model that can recover and one that is blind:
  ▎ try:
  ▎     r = httpx2.get(...)
  ▎ except Exception as e:
  ▎     raise ToolError(f"Could not reach the jobs API: {type(e).__name__}")
  ▎ An unhandled exception in an MCP tool is a silent error. That is the rule 
  ▎ worth remembering from this step.

  What changed and why: the example is now deterministic and offline-runnable
  (Finding 6), and the masking behaviour — which I verified in both directions —
  is stated instead of omitted. The step's takeaway gets sharper, not weaker:
  the reason to write ToolError stops being style advice and becomes the
  mechanism.

  ---

  C. The resources/read output — Step 7

  Original

  ▎ npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector --cli uv run server.py \
  ▎   --method resources/read --uri "learner://profile/learner_001"
  ▎ Which returns:
  ▎ {
  ▎   "name": "Anil",
  ▎   "skill": "Generative AI",
  ▎   "location": "Hyderabad"
  ▎ }

  Revised

  ▎ npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector --cli uv run server.py \
  ▎   --method resources/read --uri "learner://profile/learner_001"
  ▎ Which returns:
  ▎ {
  ▎   "contents": [
  ▎     {
  ▎       "uri": "learner://profile/learner_001",
  ▎       "mimeType": "application/json",
  ▎       "text": "{\n  \"name\": \"Anil\",\n  \"skill\": \"Generative AI\",\n  
  ▎ \"location\": \"Hyderabad\"\n}"
  ▎     }
  ▎   ]
  ▎ }
  ▎ You get an envelope, not your dictionary. Three things in it are worth 
  ▎ reading:
  ▎
  ▎ - contents is an array — one URI is allowed to return several pieces of 
  ▎ content.
  ▎ - mimeType is the one we declared on the decorator. This is where it 
  ▎ surfaces.
  ▎ - Your JSON arrived as a string inside text. The protocol carries text; the 
  ▎ client parses it. That is exactly what mime_type tells the client to do.
  ▎
  ▎ This envelope is what "a resource" actually is on the wire. Worth looking at
  ▎ closely — it is the clearest view of the protocol you'll get all session.

  What changed and why: the output is now what the command actually prints
  (verified against Inspector 2.3.0), which unblocks the lab step. And the
  correction pays for itself: the envelope is the most instructive artifact in
  the session, and the original was hiding it.

  ---

  8. Additions worth making

  1. One diagram: what tools/list → tools/call actually looks like on the wire.
  The doc explains JSON-RPC in passing and never shows a message. Draw two
  columns, client and server, with four labelled arrows: initialize → initialize
  result (carrying protocolVersion: 2026-07-28 and serverInfo), then tools/list
  → the schema array, then tools/call {name, arguments} → {content, 
  structuredContent, isError}. Every concept in the doc — schema generation,
  structured output, isError, protocol negotiation, the Inspector's --method
  names — hangs off that one picture, and the doc currently makes students
  assemble it from six separate places.

  2. The worked example to add: watch the schema change break a client. This
  makes Step 8's semver table concrete in about fifteen lines. Rename location
  to city in search_jobs, then re-run the Inspector call the student already
  ran:

  npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector --cli uv run server.py \
    --method tools/call --tool-name search_jobs \
    --tool-arg skill="Generative AI" --tool-arg location="Hyderabad"
  is_error=True
  content=[TextContent(type='text', text='Error executing tool search_jobs: 1
  validation error
           for search_jobsArguments\ncity\n  Field required ...')]

  ▎ Nothing about your logic changed. You renamed one parameter, and every 
  ▎ caller that had discovered your old schema now fails. This is why Step 8 
  ▎ called your schemas a public contract — and why that rename is a 2.0.0, not 
  ▎ a tidy-up.

  I verified this shape of error is what the SDK produces for a missing required
  argument, so the output is real.

  3. Five self-check questions (collapse the answers in a <details>):

  1. You want the model to decide when to fetch something. Tool, resource, or
     prompt? What if you want the application to decide?
  2. Your server needs to read files from the user's laptop. Which transport,
     and why is the other one wrong?
  3. A tool hits a timeout and you let the exception propagate. What exactly
     does the model receive?
  4. You add a new optional argument to an existing tool. Minor or major version
     bump? Now make it required — what changes and why?
  5. Your stdio server starts and immediately exits with no error message. Name
     two things you'd check first.

  Answers: (1) tool; resource. (2) stdio — the server runs on that machine as a
  subprocess with the user's file access; HTTP would need the files to be
  somewhere the server can reach. (3) is_error=True and the text Error executing
  tool <name> — the tool's name and nothing else. (4) Minor, then major:
  existing calls that omit it become invalid. (5) A syntax error or import
  failure at startup (run python server.py directly and read stderr), and a
  missing dependency in the environment the client launches — the client's PATH
  isn't necessarily your shell's.

  4. A debugging exercise — "the server that won't start." MCP failures are
  unusually opaque: a stdio server that dies produces no output at all through
  the client, which is a genuinely new failure mode for students who have only
  used notebooks. Plant three faults, one at a time:

  - Add import nonexistent_module at the top → the client reports a connection
    failure with no cause. Lesson: run python server.py directly first; the 
    client hides startup errors.
  - Register two tools with the same function name → one silently wins. Lesson: 
    warn_on_duplicate_tools exists for a reason; check tools/list.
  - Return a set from a tool annotated -> list[dict] → an output-validation
    failure, masked to Error executing tool …. Lesson: Finding 1's rule again, 
    from the other direction.

  Pair it with the habit worth teaching: --method tools/list before anything 
  else. If the schema isn't what you expect, no amount of debugging the body
  will help.

  5. A ten-line "Prompts" section to close Finding 7 — @mcp.prompt(), one
  templated string, one sentence that the user picks it from a menu (unlike a
  tool, which the model picks). Even if you'd rather defer, the table promised
  three primitives.

  ---

  11. Questions for the author

  1. Which docs page is the print() quote from? I verified the claim is false
     for mcp 2.1.1 — the SDK explicitly diverts fd 1 to stderr. I'd like to
     correct the text without misrepresenting a source I couldn't locate.
  2. How long is the slot? <INSERT DURATION> was blank again, so §9 is
     proportional. The Inspector-before-errors swap matters more the shorter the
     session.
  3. Is uv the intended path? It's what the official docs use and I'd keep it —
     but it isn't installed on this machine, so Step 1 is the only step I could
     not verify end-to-end. Everything after it I ran with a plain venv and it
     behaved identically.
  4. Where does this sit in the sequence? It has no number in the filename. It
     must come after 43 (Integrating MCP) since it opens by referencing it. If
     it lands before 43, the entire framing inverts. I checked: 43 already
     teaches host/client/server, MultiServerMCPClient, "transport": "http", and
     both transports — so this doc correctly deepens rather than repeats,
     provided it comes after.
  5. Was mcp_code/transcript.txt captured in one run? Its failing-search_jobs
     line shows the full ToolError message, and I reproduced that — but I also
     got a run where the message was stripped to Error executing tool 
     search_jobs. If the transcript is a composite of several runs, that's worth
     knowing, because the flakiness is the thing Finding 6 fixes.
  6. Do you want Prompts built, or explicitly deferred? Either is defensible;
     silence isn't, since the primitives table promises three.
  7. Should the security note stay? Origin validation and DNS-rebinding are
     correct but unexplained, in a doc that explains everything else. My
     instinct is to cut to "add authentication before exposing this publicly"
     and defer — but if MCP security is coming later in the course, a forward
     pointer would be better than a cut.

● Everything verified against a running server. Here's what changed.

  Critical

  1. Step 8's lesson now matches its own code. The doc taught "raise ToolError
  with a descriptive message" while leaving every httpx2 call unguarded — so a
  network failure produced Error executing tool search_jobs with the reason
  stripped. I verified both halves: unguarded → masked, ToolError → full
  message. All three tools now wrap their HTTP call, and a new subsection "The 
  Rule Most Tutorials Skip" states what was missing:

  ▎ An unhandled exception in an MCP tool is a silent error.

  2. The print() warning was false for the SDK the doc mandates. mcp 2.x takes a
  private copy of stdout for the protocol and redirects your program's stdout
  to stderr (_open_stdout_diversion → os.dup(2)). I tested a print() inside a
  tool and at import time — neither broke anything. Since you couldn't source
  the "official documentation" quote, I removed it rather than re-attribute it.
  The advice survives with three honest reasons; the false claim and the
  unsourceable quote are gone.

  3. resources/read now shows the real envelope — contents[] with uri, mimeType,
  and your JSON as an escaped string in text. The doc had shown the payload.
  This was the best teaching artifact in the session and it was being hidden, so
  the correction pays for itself.

  4. Identity. save_learner_profile takes user_id from the model, which reverses
  Session 33's lesson. I didn't change the code — it's fine over stdio — but
  added a warning naming why, and the rule: an MCP tool argument is something 
  the model can choose; identity is not.

  Major

  Prompts added (you said "best suitable" — I built it). @mcp.prompt() with
  career_review, verified output, and the line that completes the ownership
  triad: Tools do work. Resources supply data. Prompts supply intent. Wired into
  the overview, final code, and summary.

  Inspector moved before the error section. Step 8's claims are all about what a
  client observes; they were asserted several pages before students had any way
  to look. Steps renumbered, and the Inspector's closing note now leads
  straight into errors.

  Failure demo made deterministic — it depended on JSearch returning nothing for
  "Atlantis", and I got a run where it returned a job. Now: unset the API key.

  structured_content showed a string result under an array schema. Both tools'
  outputs now shown, correctly labelled. Plus: sync-blocking named as an
  explicit deferral, uv Windows install line, [cli] extra explained, security
  note made self-contained (nothing later in the course teaches it — I checked).

  Additions

  - A wire-level sequence diagram (mcp-wire-messages.png) showing all four
    exchanges. The doc made students assemble this from six places; it now sits
    right after the JSON-RPC definition and pre-explains every --method in
    Step 7.
  - "See It Break" — rename location to city, re-run the Inspector call that
    worked, watch validation fail. Makes "schemas are a public contract"
    concrete in thirty seconds. Real captured error.
  - "When Things Go Wrong" — symptom→where-to-look table plus a three-fault
    exercise. All three verified, and one is nastier than I'd guessed: a
    duplicate tool name means the first definition wins with no warning at all,
    despite warn_on_duplicate_tools defaulting to True.
  - Five self-check questions with answers.
  - Defined on first use: subprocess, JSON-RPC, stdout/stderr, semver.

  On the companion code

  Since you hadn't run it, transcript.txt was written rather than captured and
  the README asserted a verification that hadn't happened — which is exactly
  where the two wrong outputs came from. Both fixed: server.py carries the same
  guards and prompt (verified AST-identical to the doc's final code block),
  test_server.py now covers prompts and both error paths, and transcript.txt is
  regenerated captured output with a header saying so. The README now records
  what was actually checked and, explicitly, what wasn't.

  Still unverified: the uv flow in Step 1 — uv isn't installed here, so I ran
  everything with ./.venv/bin/python. Everything downstream behaves identically,
  and that's now noted in the README rather than glossed over.

  Doc went 716 → 983 lines. 16 Python blocks, tags balanced, all four images
  resolve. The one "syntax error" my checker reports is the deliberate one-line

● Answers to all three, with the checks I ran.

  ---

  1. Which use case, and does it get out of the way?

  The use case is skillmap — the same SkillMap Agent they've carried since 
  Session 29. Three tools (skill_demand, search_jobs, save_learner_profile), one
  resource (learner://profile/{user_id}), one prompt (career_review).

  This is the right choice, and it's the doc's strongest structural decision.
  The domain is already in their heads from Sessions 29, 31 and 43, so every bit
  of new thinking goes into MCP. The doc says so explicitly:

  ▎ "The function bodies are the same code from the SkillMap Agent. Only the 
  ▎ decorator and the process boundary are new."

  That sentence is the whole pedagogical strategy, and the arc table at the top
  reinforces it: wrote @tool → consumed someone else's server → publish your
  own. A student is never learning a domain and a protocol at the same time.

  But there is one real tax on "connect easily", and it isn't conceptual — it's 
  API keys. I checked: 2 of the 3 tools refuse to run without a key (Tavily and
  RapidAPI). A student without both gets a working server whose tools all error
  out. That's the friction point in this session, not MCP.

  Two things soften it, one of which I already added:

  - Step 2's minimal server is keyless (return f"Demand information for 
    {skill}") — a student reaches a working tools/list with zero setup. Good.
  - The failure demo now deliberately unsets a key, so a keyless student sees
    the intended output rather than a broken lab.

  The fix worth making: keep one tool keyless in the final server too.
  save_learner_profile already is — it just writes JSON to disk. I'd say so
  explicitly, so a student with no keys still has a complete round trip: call a
  tool, write state, read it back through a resource, fire a prompt. Then the
  two API tools become the "now make it real" step rather than a prerequisite.

  One small continuity slip I found: the doc says the bodies are "the same
  code," but search_jobs quietly drops employment_types and job_requirements
  from Session 31's version, and switches requests → httpx2. Harmless, but if a
  student diffs against their old notebook they'll notice. Either restore the
  two params or soften the claim to "the same logic."

  ---

  2. Is it enough to build their own server?

  For a server shaped like this one — yes, now. They have: setup, server object,
  all three primitives, schema generation, both transports, the error model,
  structured output, testing in three modes, versioning, and registration with
  two different clients. Plus the debugging table, exercise and self-checks I
  added.

  For an arbitrary use case — there are four gaps. These are the things a
  student hits on day one of their own project:

  Gap: Writing a good tool description
  Why it bites: The docstring is the model's only guide to when and how to call
  the tool. The doc says the docstring becomes the description and stops there.
   This single skill decides whether their server works well or badly, and it
  costs a paragraph.
  Fix: Add ~6 lines to Step 3: name the tool as a verb, say when to use it in
  the
  docstring, name units and formats. Show one weak docstring next to one good
  one.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gap: Constraining inputs beyond a type hint
  Why it bites: skill: str accepts anything. Real tools need enums, ranges,
  defaults. Pydantic Field annotations flow straight into the published schema
  — the highest-value technique the doc doesn't mention.
  Fix: One example: location: str = Field(description="City name, e.g. 
  Hyderabad") and show it appearing in inputSchema.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gap: Dependencies at launch time
  Why it bites: Their server will need libraries beyond httpx2. When a client
  launches it as a subprocess, it uses that environment. This is the most
  common real-world MCP failure and it's now in the debugging table, but
  there's no positive guidance.
  Fix: One line in Step 1: uv add <your-library>, and note that the client must
  launch the server through the same environment — which is what uv run is
  doing for them.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gap: A blank skeleton to start from
  Why it bites: They have a worked example, not a starting point.
  Fix: A 12-line "your own server" stub at the end: imports, MCPServer(...), one

  empty @mcp.tool(), mcp.run().

  Two smaller ones: the doc never says where to put .env — I tested bare
  load_dotenv() and it correctly resolves relative to the script even when the
  client launches from a different directory, so the pattern is sound, it just
  needs one sentence saying "next to server.py." And lifespan (open a DB
  connection once at startup) isn't mentioned, which is fine to defer but worth
  a pointer for anyone whose use case isn't stateless.

  Add those and a student can genuinely go build their own.

  ---

  3. Is this the global standard, or did we do something different?

  Mostly standard — with one thing you're ahead of the world on, and one thing 
  that's local to this doc.

  Standard, no caveats: MCP itself; the official Python SDK;
  decorator-registered tools with schema-from-type-hints; stdio + Streamable
  HTTP; the MCP Inspector as the testing tool; claude mcp add; treating
  published schemas as a versioned contract. Someone arriving from any other MCP
  tutorial will recognise all of it.

  Where you're ahead: mcp 2.x is new, and almost everything online is still 1.x.
  In 1.x the class was FastMCP; in 2.x it's MCPServer. This isn't a guess — the
  SDK raises it as an error message itself:

  No module named 'mcp.server.fastmcp'. This is mcp 2.x, where FastMCP was
  renamed to
  MCPServer ... see the migration guide ... or pin 'mcp<2' to keep running v1
  code.

  Your doc's closing note already warns about this, which is exactly right and
  I'd keep it prominent — a student who googles will land on 1.x code that
  doesn't import. Related: httpx2 looks wrong and isn't; I checked, and mcp 
  2.1.1 depends on it directly (Required-by: mcp). That's a 2.x-ism most Python
  developers haven't seen.

  Where this doc diverges from common practice — three things, all defensible 
  but worth naming:

  1. Using httpx2 for your own API calls. The doc's reasoning ("it's already
     installed") is fine for a lab, but most real servers use httpx or requests
     and treat the SDK's dependency as an internal detail. One caveat line would
     prevent students carrying this into their own projects.
  2. Sync def tools. Most production MCP servers use async def. I've named this
     as an explicit deferral with the reason, which I think is the right call
     for this audience — but it is a divergence from what they'll see in the
     wild.
  3. mcp.run(transport="sse") listed as a co-equal third option. SSE is the
     older HTTP transport that Streamable HTTP replaced. I found no deprecation
     warning in the SDK, so — UNVERIFIED, worth confirming against the current 
     spec — but at minimum the doc presents three equal choices when really it's
     two current ones plus a legacy one. A half-line ("sse is the older HTTP
     transport, kept for compatibility") would set the expectation correctly.

  Storing state in a local JSON file is also not typical production practice,
  but it's obviously a teaching simplification and the doc doesn't pretend
  otherwise.

  ---

  Short version: the use case is well chosen and gets out of the way — API keys
  are the friction, not MCP. The session is complete for building this server
  and needs about four small additions to be complete for building their server,
  of which tool-description guidance is the highest value. And the approach is
  mainstream, with the honest caveat that mcp 2.x is new enough that the
  internet hasn't caught up — which your existing FastMCP note handles, and
  which is worth keeping loud.

  Want me to make the six additions from Q1 and Q2? They're all small — roughly
  a paragraph each, plus the skeleton.

  The agent's actual reply after saving through the server: "Nice to meet you, 
  Meera! I have successfully saved your profile."

  The Step 8 fix, proven with a live model

  This is the payoff of the guard I added. Same failure, two code paths:

  Guarded (ToolError)
  Tool result the model received: Error executing tool search_jobs: 
  RAPID_API_KEY
   is not configured on the server.
  What the model told the user: "I was unable to retrieve the job listings… 
  because the necessary API key for the search tool  is not configured on the 
  server."
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Unguarded (raw exception)
  Tool result the model received: Error executing tool unguarded_jobs
  What the model told the user: "it failed with an error."

  One try/except is the difference between a diagnosis and a shrug. Worth
  putting this comparison in the doc verbatim — it's more persuasive than the
  explanation.

  New Critical finding: Step 10 cannot run in the server's own environment

  langchain-mcp-adapters 0.3.2 requires mcp<2.0.0,>=1.24.0. The doc's Step 1
  mandates mcp 2.x, and the whole server is written against it.

  A student who reaches Step 10 and runs uv add langchain-mcp-adapters in their
  skillmap-mcp project gets this — I dry-ran it:

  Collecting mcp<2.0.0,>=1.24.0 (from langchain-mcp-adapters)
    Downloading mcp-1.29.1-py3-none-any.whl

  pip silently downgrades mcp 2.1.1 → 1.29.1, and from mcp.server import 
  MCPServer stops importing. Their working server breaks at the last step of the
  session, and the error points at the server, not at the install.

  It does work in two environments — that's how I ran it: client venv with mcp
  1.29.1 + adapters + gemini-3.1-flash-lite, launching the mcp-2.x server as a
  subprocess. And that's not a workaround, it's the point of the protocol. Step
  10 should say so:

  ▎ Install the client library in a separate environment — not in your server 
  ▎ project. langchain-mcp-adapters still requires SDK 1.x, and adding it here 
  ▎ would downgrade the 2.x SDK your server needs.
  ▎
  ▎ That this works at all is the protocol earning its keep: a 1.x client is 
  ▎ talking to a 2.x server, across a major version boundary, because neither 
  ▎ one imports the other. They only agree on the messages.

  That's a better ending than the current one, and it's true — I ran it.

  Two smaller additions from the run

  Step 10 undersells the client. MultiServerMCPClient also has get_resources()
  and get_prompt(), both verified against your server:

  res  = await client.get_resources("skillmap",
  uris=["learner://profile/learner_001"])
  msgs = await client.get_prompt("skillmap", "career_review",
                                 arguments={"skill": "Generative AI",
  "location": "Pune"})
  The resource comes back as a Blob, the prompt as a HumanMessage list. Two
  lines would show students that all three primitives they built are reachable,
  not just tools.

  A claim I suspected and was wrong about: I thought bare load_dotenv() would
  fail when a client launches the server from a different directory. I tested it
  — python-dotenv resolves relative to the script, not the cwd, so it works. No
  change needed; the doc should just say to put .env next to server.py.

  My runs wrote three test profiles into mcp_code/profiles.json; I've restored
  it to just learner_001 and removed the temporary server variants.
  transcript.txt is real captured output. The client venv lives in scratchpad,
  not in your project.

     1. I removed a citation that turned out to be real. In the earlier pass I
        deleted the
        "From the official documentation" quote about print() because it could
        not be sourced
        and I had verified the behaviour is no longer true in mcp 2.1.1.
        reference_URLs.md
        §5 names the source, and fetching it confirms the quote is verbatim and
        current — it sits
        on the 2026-07-28 revision of
        https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/develop/build-server:

     ▎ "For STDIO-based servers: Never write to stdout. Writing to stdout will 
     corrupt the
     ▎ JSON-RPC messages and break your server. The print() function writes to 
     stdout by
     ▎ default, so keep it out of a STDIO server entirely."

        So this is a docs-vs-implementation divergence, not a fabricated
        citation. The SDK
        guards stdout (mcp/server/stdio.py _open_stdout_diversion → os.dup(2));
        the official
        docs still state the absolute rule and say nothing about the guard. The
        doc should carry
        both facts with the citation restored.
     2. The doc follows the official quickstart exactly — confirmed line for
        line:

        "mcp[cli]",

        approach"
        question: it is the current documented approach, not a local invention.

  What changed

  1. Restored the citation I'd wrongly removed. reference_URLs.md named the
  source, and fetching it confirmed the quote is verbatim and current on the
  2026-07-28 build-server page. So my earlier removal was the error — the real
  issue is that the docs and the implementation disagree. The warning now
  carries the quote with the URL, then what actually happens ("the SDK is ahead
  of its own documentation"), then four reasons to follow the rule regardless.
  Net: the student gets a sourced rule and an accurate description of behaviour,
  which is strictly better than either version alone.

  2. Fixed Step 10's dependency conflict — the last outstanding Critical. A
  warning box before the client code, with the actual pip resolution output
  showing mcp-1.29.1 being pulled in, and the consequence spelled out: from 
  mcp.server import MCPServer stops importing and the error points at the
  server, not the install.

  3. Step 10 now shows all three primitives. Added get_resources() and
  get_prompt() — I ran both snippets exactly as written: res[0].data is a str of
  the profile JSON, get_prompt returns a HumanMessage list.

  4. sse reframed as legacy — and this one I could confirm after all. The
  current spec revision defines exactly two standard transports, stdio and
  Streamable HTTP; HTTP+SSE isn't among them. Added with a precision note, since
  Streamable HTTP still uses server-sent events for streaming replies — what
  went away was SSE as a separate transport, not the technology.

  5. reference_URLs.md corrected. §5 now records the build-server page as
  re-fetched and confirmed line-for-line (MCPServer, uv add "mcp[cli]",
  mcp.run(transport="stdio")), plus a "do not re-litigate this" note on the
  stdout divergence. §6's provenance is now dated and honest, with seven new
  rows — including the masking behaviour, the mcp<2 conflict, cross-version
  interop, and gemini-3.1-flash-lite — and an explicit "not verified: the uv
  flow" line.

  mcp_code/README.md documents the two-venv setup with a copy-pasteable recipe.
