❯ # ROLE                                                                        
  You are a senior AI engineer (15+ years in software, 4+ building production   
  LLM agent systems                                                             
  with retrieval and memory layers) who also teaches Gen AI to undergraduate    
  students. You                                                                 
  review training material with two hats on at once: the practitioner who       
  checks whether the                                                            
  technical content is correct and current, and the instructor who checks       
  whether a 2nd-year                                                            
  B.Tech student can actually walk out of the session able to build the thing.  
                                                                                
  # DOCUMENT UNDER REVIEW                                                       
  - Type: TR Doc (training resource for a live/recorded session)                
  - Session: Building Agents with Memory                                        
  - Sub-topic under review: Long-term Memory                                    
  - Audience: 2nd-year B.Tech students. Assume they ALREADY know: what Gen AI   
  and LLMs are,                                                                 
    n8n (visual workflow building), building basic LLM applications,            
  tool/function calling,                                                        
    and the concept of agents.                                                  
  - Assume they DO NOT reliably know: embeddings and vector spaces, vector      
  databases,                                                                    
    similarity search, chunking, database schema design, async/queue patterns,  
  evaluation                                                                    
    methodology, or production cost/latency reasoning.                          
  - Purpose: teach one sub-topic well enough that students can implement it in  
  a hands-on lab.                                                               
  - Document follows the ---DOC--- marker.                                      
                                                                                
  # WHAT I WANT                                                                 
  A rigorous but constructive review that makes this document (a) technically   
  accurate and                                                                  
  current, and (b) genuinely teachable to the audience above. Every criticism   
  must come with a                                                              
  concrete fix. Do not rewrite the whole document.                              
                                                                                
  # HOW TO REVIEW — PASSES IN ORDER                                             
  Pass 1 — Student comprehension: Read once as a 2nd-year student with exactly  
  the prior                                                                     
  knowledge listed. Mark every point where you'd stall, re-read, or need to     
  search elsewhere.                                                             
  Record these before critiquing as an expert.                                  
  Pass 2 — Technical audit: Verify claims, terminology, code, diagrams,         
  tool/framework                                                                
  references and version currency. Check internal consistency across sections.  
  Pass 3 — Pedagogy: Does the sequence build from what they know (agents, tool  
  calling) to                                                                   
  what's new (persistent memory)? Is each concept motivated by a problem before 
  it's named?                                                                   
  Pass 4 — Lab readiness: Could a student actually build this from the doc? Is  
  there a runnable                                                              
  path end to end?                                                              
  Pass 5 — Language & presentation: jargon load, sentence length,               
  figure/caption quality,                                                       
  formatting, attribution.                                                      
                                                                                
  # TOPIC-SPECIFIC CONTENT CHECKLIST                                            
  Mark each as Covered well / Covered weakly / Missing / Wrong, and say whether 
  it belongs at                                                                 
  this level or should be explicitly deferred.                                  
                                                                                
  Conceptual foundation                                                         
  - The motivating failure: a concrete before/after showing an agent that       
  forgets, and why                                                              
    that breaks the user experience.                                            
  - Short-term (context window / conversation buffer) vs long-term (persisted   
  across sessions)                                                              
    — is the boundary drawn crisply, with a decision rule for which to use?     
  - The distinction between long-term memory and RAG over a document corpus.    
  This is the single                                                            
    most commonly muddled point — check it explicitly.                          
  - Memory categories, if introduced: semantic (facts about the user/world),    
  episodic (past                                                                
    interactions/events), procedural (learned how-to or instructions). Are      
  these introduced with                                                         
    examples, or just as a taxonomy to memorise?                                
                                                                                
  Mechanics                                                                     
  - Write path: what triggers a memory write, what gets extracted vs stored     
  raw, who decides                                                              
    (LLM-as-extractor vs explicit tool call vs rules).                          
  - Storage: what actually sits in the database — schema, metadata, namespacing 
  per user.                                                                     
  - Retrieval path: query formulation, semantic search vs keyword vs hybrid,    
  top-k, metadata                                                               
    filtering, recency weighting, relevance thresholds.                         
  - Injection: how retrieved memories enter the prompt, where in the prompt,    
  and the token                                                                 
    budget tradeoff.                                                            
  - Maintenance: deduplication, conflict resolution when a new fact contradicts 
  an old one,                                                                   
    updates vs appends, decay/forgetting, summarisation/consolidation.          
  - Embeddings and vector search: given the audience, is there enough intuition 
  (not maths) to                                                                
    make retrieval non-magical? Or is it hand-waved?                            
                                                                                
  Practice                                                                      
  - Tool/function-calling framing: is long-term memory presented as save_memory 
  / search_memory                                                               
    tools the agent invokes? This connects directly to prior knowledge and      
  should be exploited.                                                          
  - n8n grounding: the audience knows n8n, so is there at least one worked      
  example in that                                                               
    vocabulary (memory node, vector store node, workflow shape) before or       
  alongside any code?                                                           
  - Tooling named: is it current and are choices justified rather than listed?  
  (e.g. vector                                                                  
    stores, LangGraph/LangChain memory, Mem0, Zep, Letta, plain                 
  Postgres+pgvector.) Flag                                                      
    anything deprecated, renamed, or version-sensitive.                         
  - Working code or workflow that runs, with imports, environment setup and     
  expected output.                                                              
  - Failure modes and debugging: retrieving irrelevant memories, stale facts,   
  memory pollution,                                                             
    prompt bloat, latency.                                                      
  - Cost, latency and scale reasoning at a beginner-appropriate level.          
  - Privacy and safety: PII in memory, consent, deletion/right-to-forget,       
  multi-user isolation,                                                         
    prompt injection via poisoned memory. Flag if absent — it usually is.       
  - Evaluation: how do you know the memory layer is working? Any measurable     
  notion at all.                                                                
                                                                                
  # EVALUATION DIMENSIONS (score 1–5 with a one-line justification)             
  1. Technical accuracy and currency                                            
  2. Conceptual completeness (per checklist above)                              
  3. Prerequisite calibration — does it correctly assume what they know and     
  correctly teach                                                               
     what they don't? Flag both over-explaining known material and              
  under-explaining new material.                                                
  4. Logical scaffolding — problem before solution, concrete before abstract    
  5. Clarity and jargon control for a 2nd-year reader                           
  6. Hands-on implementability                                                  
  7. Learning aids — diagrams, worked examples, analogies, summaries,           
  self-checks                                                                   
  8. Presentation, formatting and attribution                                   
                                                                                
  # OUTPUT FORMAT                                                               
  ## 1. Verdict                                                                 
  3–4 sentences: overall quality, readiness (Reject / Major revision / Minor    
  revision / Ready),                                                            
  and the single highest-leverage change.                                       
                                                                                
  ## 2. Scorecard                                                               
  Table: Dimension | Score /5 | Justification. Plus an overall score.           
                                                                                
  ## 3. Content coverage matrix                                                 
  The topic-specific checklist as a table: Item | Status | Comment | Belongs at 
  this level?                                                                   
                                                                                
  ## 4. Strengths                                                               
  3–5 specific things to preserve, with locations. Concrete, not flattering.    
                                                                                
  ## 5. Findings                                                                
  Table: # | Location | Issue | Severity (Critical / Major / Minor / Nitpick) | 
  Impact on a                                                                   
  2nd-year student | Recommended fix. Ordered by severity. Critical = wrong, or 
  blocks                                                                        
  comprehension, or breaks the lab.                                             
                                                                                
  ## 6. Student-stall points                                                    
  Pass 1 output: exact places a target student loses the thread, and what to    
  add, define or                                                                
  reorder to prevent it. Include any term used before it is defined.            
                                                                                
  ## 7. Rewrite examples                                                        
  The 3 weakest passages: Original → Revised → what changed and why. Preserve   
  the author's voice.                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 8. Additions worth making                                                  
  Be concrete and specific to this topic. For example: describe the             
  write-path/read-path diagram                                                  
  you'd draw, sketch the one worked example you'd add, propose an analogy for   
  embeddings that                                                               
  doesn't break, draft 4–5 self-check questions, suggest one debugging          
  exercise.                                                                     
                                                                                
  ## 9. Suggested session flow                                                  
  If the ordering is off, propose a revised outline with rough time allocation  
  for a                                                                         
  <INSERT DURATION>-minute slot, marking what to cut if time runs short.        
                                                                                
  ## 10. Prioritised action list                                                
  Numbered and sequenced, each tagged quick / medium / substantial. Max 10      
  items.                                                                        
                                                                                
  ## 11. Questions for the author                                               
  Anything you could not judge without more information.                        
                                                                                
  # RULES                                                                       
  - Evidence over impression: cite the section or line for every criticism.     
  Keep quotes short.                                                            
  - Never invent facts, APIs, benchmarks or citations. Mark anything you cannot 
  verify as                                                                     
    "UNVERIFIED — author to confirm" rather than guessing.                      
  - Flag any API, library name or feature that may have changed, and say what   
  to re-check.                                                                  
  - Separate "this is wrong" from "this is my preference" and label which you   
  mean.                                                                         
  - Judge against a 2nd-year B.Tech reader with the stated prerequisites — not  
  against a                                                                     
    senior engineer, and not against a total beginner.                          
  - Do not inflate scores to be encouraging, and do not soften Critical         
  findings.

  0. Reviewer's note on bias

  I edited most of this document earlier in this session, so this is partly a
  review of my own work. I've weighted it toward finding problems I introduced
  or missed, and I re-verified rather than trusting my earlier conclusions. The 
  Introduction and "What We Are Building" were rewritten by someone else at 
  16:14 — after my edits — and that rewrite is where the most serious finding
  sits. Note also that the review brief still carries the memory-session header
  and its memory-specific checklist; I've reviewed the actual document and used
  the MCP checklist variant.

  ---

  1. Verdict

  The rewritten Introduction is a genuine improvement — the all_tools = 
  mcp_tools + [search_jobs] hook is verified verbatim in Session 43 and turns an
  abstract topic into a concrete grievance the student already has. The body is
  technically sound: I re-ran the server, both transports, all three
  primitives, the Inspector, and a live cross-version agent, and everything the
  doc claims holds. But the same rewrite deleted the doc's single best passage —
  the primitives-and-ownership table — and replaced it with a deferral to a
  session that does not teach it. Readiness: Major revision, on one item.
  Highest-leverage change: restore the ownership explanation to this document,
  because everything from Step 4 onward now rests on a foundation the student
  was never given.

  4. Strengths

  1. L18–52, the + hook. Not just rhetorically good — verified. all_tools = 
     mcp_tools + [search_jobs] appears four times in Session 43. Building the
     session on a line students actually typed, then making the payoff "that
     line stops needing a +", is the best framing device in either doc I've
     reviewed. Preserve exactly.
  2. L54–62, the arc table with the "Who could use it" column. Wrote @tool →
     that one agent. Consumed someone else's → that one agent. Publish our own →
     anything that speaks MCP. The added third column is what makes the arc
     land.
  3. Step 8's "The Rule Most Tutorials Skip." I re-confirmed both branches with
     a live gemini-3.1-flash-lite agent: guarded → "unable to retrieve the job 
     listings because the necessary API key … is not configured on the server";
     unguarded → "it failed with an error." The doc's claim is exactly right,
     and "An unhandled exception in an MCP tool is a silent error" is the
     sentence students should leave with.
  4. Step 10's cross-version payoff. A 1.x client consuming a 2.x server,
     verified by running it. "They never share a Python process… the only thing
     they agree on is the messages" is a better ending than the original.
  5. The four diagrams are all accurate. Re-checked -32602 against the SDK — the
     error-paths diagram is right. Diagram/text drift is the usual failure here
     and there is none.

  5. Findings

  #: 1
  Location: L95–107, "Recall: Which Primitive, and Why"
  Issue: Defers foundational material to a session that doesn't teach it. The
  text claims "The MCP I session covered the three primitives and the one thing
   that separates them — who decides to use them." Verified false. Session 43
  contains only: "MCP standardizes how AI applications interact  with external 
  systems: Prompts / Tools / Resources" — three bullets, no explanation. I
  grepped both MCP sessions for control language (model.*decides,
  application.*decide, user.*invoke, controlled by): zero  hits. The doc then
  says "revisit that section before continuing" — pointing at a section that
  does not exist. Worse, the previous version of this doc taught this properly,
   with the ownership table and the line "A tool is something the model decides
   to do. A resource is something  the application decides to show it." — which
   I flagged as the doc's single best passage. The rewrite deleted it.
  Severity: Critical
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: This is the foundation for Step 4 ("so it is a
  resource"), Step 5 ("The third belongs to the user"), Step 5's closing note
  ("This is why the ownership column mattered"), and self-check Q1. A student
  who follows the instruction to revisit finds three bullets and comes back no
  wiser. Every downstream "because it's a resource" becomes assertion.
  Recommended fix: Restore ~12 lines. Keep the new table but make it teach
  rather
  than recall: add the third column back (What it is), the one-sentence rule —
  "A tool is something the model decides to  do. A resource is something the 
  application decides to show  it." — and change the framing from "The MCP I
  session covered…" to "Session 43 named the three primitives. Here is the
  thing it did not say: they differ by who decides to use  them." That is both
  accurate and a better hook.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Location: L97 vs L10, L60, L750
  Issue: Naming inconsistency. The same session is called "Integrating MCP"
  three
  times and "MCP I" once. No session is named "MCP I" anywhere in the corpus —
  the only match is 43-integrating-mcp.md.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: A student looking for "MCP I" in their materials

  finds nothing and cannot follow the instruction to revisit it.
  Recommended fix: Use "Integrating MCP" everywhere. If the course renames
  sessions to "MCP I / MCP II", rename all four occurrences and check the
  sibling docs.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Location: Whole doc
  Issue: No guidance on writing a tool description. The docstring is the model's

  only instruction for when and how to call the tool, and the doc says only
  that it "becomes the tool's description."
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: The single skill that most determines whether
  their own server works well, and it's absent. They'll write """Search 
  jobs.""" and wonder why the model calls it wrongly.
  Recommended fix: ~6 lines in Step 3: name the tool as a verb; say when to use
  it, not just what it does; name units and formats. Show one weak docstring
  beside one good one. Pair with a Field(description=...) example so input
  constraints reach the published schema.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Location: L413–417
  Issue: Run-on I introduced. My sse insertion merged into the next sentence:
  "…not the technology.) Running with HTTP prints:" — a parenthetical about SSE
   now shares a paragraph with the uvicorn output lead-in.
  Severity: Minor
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Momentary confusion; the parenthetical reads as
  if it belongs to the HTTP output.
  Recommended fix: Break the paragraph before "Running with HTTP prints:". My
  error.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Location: L873, final code comment
  Issue: Contradicts the revised warning. The comment still reads # stderr — 
  never print() on stdio, while Step 2 now explains the SDK guards stdout.
  Severity: Minor
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Mild inconsistency between the lesson and the
  shipped code's comment.
  Recommended fix: # stderr — keep your own output off stdout. (I made this
  change in mcp_code/server.py but missed the doc's copy.)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Location: Step 1
  Issue: No guidance on adding their own dependencies. uv add "mcp[cli]" appears

  once; a student's own server will need more, and the client launches the
  server in its environment.
  Severity: Minor
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: The most common real-world MCP failure. It's in
  the debugging table as a symptom but never as advice.
  Recommended fix: One line: uv add <your-library>, and note the client must
  launch the server through the same environment — which is what uv run does
  for them.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Location: Whole doc
  Issue: No starter skeleton. A complete worked example, but nothing to start
  from.
  Severity: Minor
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: They copy the SkillMap server and delete, rather

  than building up.
  Recommended fix: 12 lines after the final code: imports, MCPServer(...), one
  empty @mcp.tool(), mcp.run().
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Location: Step 3
  Issue: .env location never stated. load_dotenv() with no guidance on where the

  file goes. Verified: bare load_dotenv() resolves relative to the script, not
  the cwd, so it works even when a client launches from elsewhere — but the doc
   doesn't say where to put it.
  Severity: Minor
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Trial and error over something that works fine
  once stated.
  Recommended fix: One sentence: "Put .env next to server.py."
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 9
  Location: Whole doc
  Issue: Two of three tools require paid API keys. Verified: skill_demand and
  search_jobs both refuse without a key. Only save_learner_profile is keyless.
  Severity: Minor
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: A student without both keys has a working server

  whose tools all error. The Step 8 demo now deliberately unsets a key, which
  helps.
  Recommended fix: State explicitly that save_learner_profile + the resource +
  the prompt give a complete keyless round trip, and that the two API tools are
   the "make it real" step.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 10
  Location: L96 header, "Recall:"
  Issue: Preference, not error. The ##-level section is titled "What We Are
  Building" with a ### "Recall" inside it — the recall arrives after the
  deliverables table rather than before.
  Severity: Nitpick
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Slight ordering wobble.
  Recommended fix: Put the primitive explanation before the "what we're
  building"
  table, so the table's three rows are already meaningful.

  6. Student-stall points

  Where: L97, "The MCP I session covered…"
  What stalls them: The hard stall. They are told they already know something
  they don't, told to go revisit it, and find three bullet points. Then Step 4
  opens "so it is a resource" as if settled.
  Fix: Finding 1. This one stall poisons Steps 4, 5 and self-check Q1.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: L105, "the application supplies it as context"
  What stalls them: Application is doing heavy lifting — is that Claude Desktop?

  their agent? their code? Never pinned down.
  Fix: One clause: "the application — the host program, like Claude Code or your

  own agent script — decides to put it in front of the model."
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: Step 3, raise ToolError(...) before Step 8
  What stalls them: ToolError appears ~8 times in Step 3 and is only explained
  in
  Step 8. Students meet an unexplained exception type in the first real code.
  Fix: One line in Step 3: "ToolError is how a tool reports a failure the model
  should see — Step 8 explains why that matters." Forward pointer, not a full
  explanation.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: Step 6, "Bearer tokens, OAuth, headers"
  What stalls them: Three auth mechanisms in a table cell, none introduced, none

  used.
  Fix: Reduce to "a token the client sends with each request" and let the
  paragraph below carry the rest.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: Step 9, "See It Break"
  What stalls them: Says "re-run the same Inspector call you ran in Step 7" —
  but
  Step 7's tools/call example is search_jobs, so this works. Confirm the
  student actually ran it; if they only ran tools/list, "the one that worked"
  doesn't refer to anything.
  Fix: Add "(the tools/call one)" for precision.

  Terms used before definition: ToolError (Step 3, explained Step 8),
  application as a role (L105), bearer token (Step 6). Now correctly defined:
  subprocess, JSON-RPC, stdout/stderr, semver.

  7. Rewrite examples

  A. The Critical one — L95–107

  Original

  ▎ Recall: Which Primitive, and Why
  ▎
  ▎ The MCP I session covered the three primitives and the one thing that 
  ▎ separates them — who decides to use them:
  ▎
  ▎ | Primitive | Controlled by |
  ▎ |-----------|---------------|
  ▎ | Tools     | Model         |
  ▎ | Resources | Application   |
  ▎ | Prompts   | User          |
  ▎
  ▎ […] If any of those choices look arbitrary, revisit that section before 
  ▎ continuing.

  Revised

  ▎ Which Primitive, and Why
  ▎
  ▎ The Integrating MCP session named the three primitives — tools, resources, 
  ▎ prompts. Here is the thing it did not say, and the thing that decides which 
  ▎ one you reach for: they differ by who chooses to use them.
  ▎
  ▎ | Primitive | What it is                           | Who chooses to use it  
  ▎                                           |
  ▎ |-----------|--------------------------------------|------------------------
  ▎ -------------------------------------------|
  ▎ | Tools     | Functions that do something          | The model — it decides 
  ▎ a search is needed                         |
  ▎ | Resources | Read-only data offered as context    | The application — your 
  ▎ agent or IDE puts it in front of the model |
  ▎ | Prompts   | Templates someone deliberately picks | The user — from a menu 
  ▎ or slash-command                           |
  ▎
  ▎ One sentence to keep:
  ▎
  ▎ ▎ A tool is something the model decides to do. A resource is something the 
  ▎ application decides to show it.
  ▎
  ▎ That is the design decision behind everything below. search_jobs is a tool 
  ▎ because the model decides when to search. The learner profile is a resource 
  ▎ because the application supplies it as background — the model never "calls" 
  ▎ it. career_review is a prompt because a person picks it to start a workflow.
  ▎
  ▎ Choosing the wrong primitive is the most common mistake in a first server, 
  ▎ and it is far cheaper to fix now than after clients have connected.

  What changed and why: the false claim about the prior session is replaced with
  an accurate one that is also a better hook — "here is the thing it did not 
  say" earns the section instead of apologising for it. The ownership rule is
  restored to this document, so Steps 4, 5 and Q1 have a foundation. The
  "revisit that section" instruction, which pointed at nothing, is gone.

  ---

  B. The run-on I introduced — L413–417

  Original

  ▎ mcp.run() accepts "stdio", "streamable-http", or "sse" — but only the first 
  ▎ two are standard transports in the current specification. […]
  ▎
  ▎ (Streamable HTTP still uses server-sent events for streaming replies. What 
  ▎ went away was SSE as a separate transport, not the technology.) Running with
  ▎ HTTP prints:

  Revised

  ▎ mcp.run() accepts "stdio", "streamable-http", or "sse" — but only the first 
  ▎ two are standard transports in the current specification. "sse" is the older
  ▎ HTTP transport, kept in the SDK so servers can still talk to 
  ▎ pre-Streamable-HTTP clients. Reach for it only if something you must support
  ▎ requires it.
  ▎
  ▎ (Streamable HTTP still uses server-sent events for streaming replies. What 
  ▎ went away was SSE as a separate transport, not the technology.)
  ▎
  ▎ Running with HTTP prints:

  What changed and why: purely a paragraph break. My insertion left the
  parenthetical glued to the next sentence, so it read as if the SSE aside were
  introducing the uvicorn output. My error, one newline to fix.

  ---

  C. Missing tool-description guidance — add to Step 3

  Original

  ▎ The SDK builds it from the type hints, and the docstring becomes the tool's 
  ▎ description.

  Revised

  ▎ The SDK builds it from the type hints, and the docstring becomes the tool's 
  ▎ description.
  ▎
  ▎ Spend a moment on that docstring. It is not documentation for you — it is 
  ▎ the only instruction the model gets about when to reach for this tool. 
  ▎ Compare:
  ▎
  ▎ """Search jobs."""                                    # what does it need? 
  ▎ when?
  ▎
  ▎ """Search for real job openings requiring a specific
  ▎ skill in a location. Use when the learner asks what
  ▎ roles are available. Location is a city name."""      # a model can act on 
  ▎ this
  ▎
  ▎ Three habits: name the tool as a verb, say when to use it and not just what 
  ▎ it does, and name units and formats for anything ambiguous. You can also 
  ▎ push detail into the schema itself:
  ▎
  ▎ from pydantic import Field
  ▎
  ▎ def search_jobs(
  ▎     skill: str,
  ▎     location: str = Field(description="City name, e.g. Hyderabad"),
  ▎ ) -> list[dict]:
  ▎
  ▎ That description travels in the published inputSchema, so every client that 
  ▎ discovers your tool sees it.

  What changed and why: the doc already establishes that the docstring is
  published over a protocol; this makes the consequence actionable. It's the
  highest-value gap for the "build your own server" goal, and the Field example
  is the one technique that reaches the schema. UNVERIFIED — author to confirm
  the exact Field rendering in the emitted schema under mcp 2.1.1 before
  publishing; I verified total=False schema effects in the memory doc but did
  not test Field here.

  ---

  8. Additions worth making

  1. The starter skeleton — 12 lines after the final code, so students build up
  rather than delete down:

  from mcp.server import MCPServer
  from mcp.server.mcpserver.exceptions import ToolError

  mcp = MCPServer("your-server-name", version="0.1.0")

  @mcp.tool()
  def your_tool(argument: str) -> str:
      """Say what this does and when the model should use it."""
      try:
          ...
      except Exception as e:
          raise ToolError(f"Could not complete the request: {type(e).__name__}")
      return "result"

  if __name__ == "__main__":
      mcp.run(transport="stdio")

  The try/except → ToolError is in the skeleton deliberately — it makes the Step
  8 rule the default shape rather than something to remember.

  2. A "keyless path" callout in Step 3. Two of three tools need paid keys.
  State that save_learner_profile + the resource + the prompt give a complete
  round trip with no keys at all, so a student without Tavily/RapidAPI still
  finishes the lab.

  3. Two more self-check questions, now that prompts are covered:
  - You want a "review my résumé" workflow that a user picks from a menu, which 
    then calls two of your tools. Which primitive is the menu entry, and which 
    are the two things it triggers?
  - Your server works from the Inspector but your agent gets "connection 
    closed". Name the two environment differences most likely responsible.

  4. One line of cost reasoning where the blocking-I/O note already sits: a
  stdio server costs one process per client and starts fresh each time; an HTTP
  server is one process serving many, which is why state that must be shared
  belongs there and not in a module-level dict.

  ---

  9. Suggested session flow

  Duration is still unspecified — you've said twice to focus on content over
  timing, so this is ordering only, no minutes.

  The sequence is now genuinely good and I'd change one thing plus keep one
  earlier change:

  - Move the primitive explanation before "What We Are Building" (Finding 10).
    The deliverables table lists 3 tools / 1 resource / 1 prompt; those three
    words only mean something once ownership is explained. Currently the table
    comes first and the explanation second.
  - Keep Inspector (Step 7) before Errors (Step 8). That swap from the earlier
    pass is working — Step 8 now opens "You now have a way to watch your server
    answer. Point it at things that go wrong", which only reads correctly in
    this order.

  Everything else — + hook → why it's a problem → leaving Colab → what we're
  building → setup → smallest server → tools → resource → prompt → transports →
  testing → errors → versioning → own agent → when to build one → debugging →
  self-checks — is the right spine. No cuts recommended.

  3. What I fixed

  Critical — the ownership regression. L97 claimed "The MCP I session covered 
  the three primitives and the one thing that separates them — who decides to 
  use them." Verified false: Session 43 contains only Prompts / Tools / 
  Resources as bullets, and I grepped both MCP sessions for control language
  (model.*decides, application.*decide, controlled by) — zero hits. The doc then
  said "revisit that section", pointing at nothing. Since only this session is
  in scope, I restored the material here rather than sending students away:

  ▎ They differ by who chooses to use them.
  ▎ …
  ▎ A tool is something the model decides to do. A resource is something the 
  ▎ application decides to show it.

  Reframed as "Here is what it did not say" — accurate, and a better hook than a
  recall. Moved above "What We Are Building" so the 3-tools/1-resource/1-prompt
  table is meaningful when read, and gave that table a "Why this primitive"
  column.

  A second regression the rewrite caused: mcp-host-client-server.png had been
  orphaned — the section that used it was deleted. Restored it in the new
  primitives section, where it now illustrates "the application" directly. All
  four diagrams resolve again.

  "MCP I" → "Integrating MCP" — you confirmed no rename is happening, and no
  session by that name exists.

  Two errors of my own from the previous pass: the SSE parenthetical had run
  into "Running with HTTP prints:" (missing paragraph break), and the final code
  still carried # never print() on stdio, contradicting the warning I'd revised
  two sections earlier.

  For the stated outcome — build a server without facing issues:

  - Tool descriptions — the highest-value gap. The docstring is the model's only
    instruction, and the doc said only that it "becomes the description". Added
    a weak-vs-good comparison, three habits, and a per-argument
    Field(description=...) example. I verified both Field forms reach the
    published inputSchema and keep the argument required, and noted that despite
    the =, it isn't a default value.
  - Starter skeleton — 15 lines with the try/except → ToolError already in it,
    so the Step 8 rule is the default shape rather than something to remember.
    Version starts at 0.1.0, with the reason.
  - Their own dependencies — uv add <library>, and why it matters: the client
    launches the server as a subprocess, so deps must be where uv run starts.
    That's the "works for me but not from the agent" failure.
  - .env location — next to server.py; verified load_dotenv() resolves relative
    to the script, so it survives being launched from elsewhere.
  - ToolError forward pointer in Step 3, where it first appears eight times
    before Step 8 explains it.
  - Two more self-checks (the prompt-vs-tool trigger question; the
    Inspector-works-but-agent-fails question) and a cost note: stdio is one
    process per client thrown away each time, HTTP is one process serving many —
    which is where shared state can live.

  Dropped: the keyless-path callout, since you confirmed keys are provisioned.

❯ # ROLE                                                                        
  You are a senior AI engineer (15+ years in software, 4+ building production   
  LLM agent systems                                                             
  with retrieval and memory layers) who also teaches Gen AI to undergraduate    
  students. You                                                                 
  review training material with two hats on at once: the practitioner who       
  checks whether the                                                            
  technical content is correct and current, and the instructor who checks       
  whether a 2nd-year                                                            
  B.Tech student can actually walk out of the session able to build the thing.  
                                                                                
  # DOCUMENT UNDER REVIEW                                                       
  - Type: TR Doc (training resource for a live/recorded session)                
  - Session: Building Agents with Memory                                        
  - Sub-topic under review: Long-term Memory                                    
  - Audience: 2nd-year B.Tech students. Assume they ALREADY know: what Gen AI   
  and LLMs are,                                                                 
    n8n (visual workflow building), building basic LLM applications,            
  tool/function calling,                                                        
    and the concept of agents.                                                  
  - Assume they DO NOT reliably know: embeddings and vector spaces, vector      
  databases,                                                                    
    similarity search, chunking, database schema design, async/queue patterns,  
  evaluation                                                                    
    methodology, or production cost/latency reasoning.                          
  - Purpose: teach one sub-topic well enough that students can implement it in  
  a hands-on lab.                                                               
  - Document follows the ---DOC--- marker.                                      
                                                                                
  # WHAT I WANT                                                                 
  A rigorous but constructive review that makes this document (a) technically   
  accurate and                                                                  
  current, and (b) genuinely teachable to the audience above. Every criticism   
  must come with a                                                              
  concrete fix. Do not rewrite the whole document.                              
                                                                                
  # HOW TO REVIEW — PASSES IN ORDER                                             
  Pass 1 — Student comprehension: Read once as a 2nd-year student with exactly  
  the prior                                                                     
  knowledge listed. Mark every point where you'd stall, re-read, or need to     
  search elsewhere.                                                             
  Record these before critiquing as an expert.                                  
  Pass 2 — Technical audit: Verify claims, terminology, code, diagrams,         
  tool/framework                                                                
  references and version currency. Check internal consistency across sections.  
  Pass 3 — Pedagogy: Does the sequence build from what they know (agents, tool  
  calling) to                                                                   
  what's new (persistent memory)? Is each concept motivated by a problem before 
  it's named?                                                                   
  Pass 4 — Lab readiness: Could a student actually build this from the doc? Is  
  there a runnable                                                              
  path end to end?                                                              
  Pass 5 — Language & presentation: jargon load, sentence length,               
  figure/caption quality,                                                       
  formatting, attribution.                                                      
                                                                                
  # TOPIC-SPECIFIC CONTENT CHECKLIST                                            
  Mark each as Covered well / Covered weakly / Missing / Wrong, and say whether 
  it belongs at                                                                 
  this level or should be explicitly deferred.                                  
                                                                                
  Conceptual foundation                                                         
  - The motivating failure: a concrete before/after showing an agent that       
  forgets, and why                                                              
    that breaks the user experience.                                            
  - Short-term (context window / conversation buffer) vs long-term (persisted   
  across sessions)                                                              
    — is the boundary drawn crisply, with a decision rule for which to use?     
  - The distinction between long-term memory and RAG over a document corpus.    
  This is the single                                                            
    most commonly muddled point — check it explicitly.                          
  - Memory categories, if introduced: semantic (facts about the user/world),    
  episodic (past                                                                
    interactions/events), procedural (learned how-to or instructions). Are      
  these introduced with                                                         
    examples, or just as a taxonomy to memorise?                                
                                                                                
  Mechanics                                                                     
  - Write path: what triggers a memory write, what gets extracted vs stored     
  raw, who decides                                                              
    (LLM-as-extractor vs explicit tool call vs rules).                          
  - Storage: what actually sits in the database — schema, metadata, namespacing 
  per user.                                                                     
  - Retrieval path: query formulation, semantic search vs keyword vs hybrid,    
  top-k, metadata                                                               
    filtering, recency weighting, relevance thresholds.                         
  - Injection: how retrieved memories enter the prompt, where in the prompt,    
  and the token                                                                 
    budget tradeoff.                                                            
  - Maintenance: deduplication, conflict resolution when a new fact contradicts 
  an old one,                                                                   
    updates vs appends, decay/forgetting, summarisation/consolidation.          
  - Embeddings and vector search: given the audience, is there enough intuition 
  (not maths) to                                                                
    make retrieval non-magical? Or is it hand-waved?                            
                                                                                
  Practice                                                                      
  - Tool/function-calling framing: is long-term memory presented as save_memory 
  / search_memory                                                               
    tools the agent invokes? This connects directly to prior knowledge and      
  should be exploited.                                                          
  - n8n grounding: the audience knows n8n, so is there at least one worked      
  example in that                                                               
    vocabulary (memory node, vector store node, workflow shape) before or       
  alongside any code?                                                           
  - Tooling named: is it current and are choices justified rather than listed?  
  (e.g. vector                                                                  
    stores, LangGraph/LangChain memory, Mem0, Zep, Letta, plain                 
  Postgres+pgvector.) Flag                                                      
    anything deprecated, renamed, or version-sensitive.                         
  - Working code or workflow that runs, with imports, environment setup and     
  expected output.                                                              
  - Failure modes and debugging: retrieving irrelevant memories, stale facts,   
  memory pollution,                                                             
    prompt bloat, latency.                                                      
  - Cost, latency and scale reasoning at a beginner-appropriate level.          
  - Privacy and safety: PII in memory, consent, deletion/right-to-forget,       
  multi-user isolation,                                                         
    prompt injection via poisoned memory. Flag if absent — it usually is.       
  - Evaluation: how do you know the memory layer is working? Any measurable     
  notion at all.                                                                
                                                                                
  # EVALUATION DIMENSIONS (score 1–5 with a one-line justification)             
  1. Technical accuracy and currency                                            
  2. Conceptual completeness (per checklist above)                              
  3. Prerequisite calibration — does it correctly assume what they know and     
  correctly teach                                                               
     what they don't? Flag both over-explaining known material and              
  under-explaining new material.                                                
  4. Logical scaffolding — problem before solution, concrete before abstract    
  5. Clarity and jargon control for a 2nd-year reader                           
  6. Hands-on implementability                                                  
  7. Learning aids — diagrams, worked examples, analogies, summaries,           
  self-checks                                                                   
  8. Presentation, formatting and attribution                                   
                                                                                
  # OUTPUT FORMAT                                                               
  ## 1. Verdict                                                                 
  3–4 sentences: overall quality, readiness (Reject / Major revision / Minor    
  revision / Ready),                                                            
  and the single highest-leverage change.                                       
                                                                                
  ## 2. Scorecard                                                               
  Table: Dimension | Score /5 | Justification. Plus an overall score.           
                                                                                
  ## 3. Content coverage matrix                                                 
  The topic-specific checklist as a table: Item | Status | Comment | Belongs at 
  this level?                                                                   
                                                                                
  ## 4. Strengths                                                               
  3–5 specific things to preserve, with locations. Concrete, not flattering.    
                                                                                
  ## 5. Findings                                                                
  Table: # | Location | Issue | Severity (Critical / Major / Minor / Nitpick) | 
  Impact on a                                                                   
  2nd-year student | Recommended fix. Ordered by severity. Critical = wrong, or 
  blocks                                                                        
  comprehension, or breaks the lab.                                             
                                                                                
  ## 6. Student-stall points                                                    
  Pass 1 output: exact places a target student loses the thread, and what to    
  add, define or                                                                
  reorder to prevent it. Include any term used before it is defined.            
                                                                                
  ## 7. Rewrite examples                                                        
  The 3 weakest passages: Original → Revised → what changed and why. Preserve   
  the author's voice.                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 8. Additions worth making                                                  
  Be concrete and specific to this topic. For example: describe the             
  write-path/read-path diagram                                                  
  you'd draw, sketch the one worked example you'd add, propose an analogy for   
  embeddings that                                                               
  doesn't break, draft 4–5 self-check questions, suggest one debugging          
  exercise.                                                                     
                                                                                
  ## 9. Suggested session flow                                                  
  If the ordering is off, propose a revised outline with rough time allocation  
  for a                                                                         
  <INSERT DURATION>-minute slot, marking what to cut if time runs short.        
                                                                                
  ## 10. Prioritised action list                                                
  Numbered and sequenced, each tagged quick / medium / substantial. Max 10      
  items.                                                                        
                                                                                
  ## 11. Questions for the author                                               
  Anything you could not judge without more information.                        
                                                                                
  # RULES                                                                       
  - Evidence over impression: cite the section or line for every criticism.     
  Keep quotes short.                                                            
  - Never invent facts, APIs, benchmarks or citations. Mark anything you cannot 
  verify as                                                                     
    "UNVERIFIED — author to confirm" rather than guessing.                      
  - Flag any API, library name or feature that may have changed, and say what   
  to re-check.                                                                  
  - Separate "this is wrong" from "this is my preference" and label which you   
  mean.                                                                         
  - Judge against a 2nd-year B.Tech reader with the stated prerequisites — not  
  against a                                                                     
    senior engineer, and not against a total beginner.                          
  - Do not inflate scores to be encouraging, and do not soften Critical         
  findings.

  ---

  1. Verdict

  The prose is clear and the problem-first arc works, but two things dominate
  everything else: this document substantially duplicates the existing Session 
  41, in places word-for-word and in several places with less rigor than what's
  already published; and it contains no runnable code or workflow at all,
  against a stated purpose of "students can implement it in a hands-on lab."
  Readiness: Major revision — but the revision needed is editorial-strategic,
  not line-level. Highest-leverage change: decide what this document is relative
  to Session 41 before improving it any further, because roughly 80% of the
  content already exists and fixing the other 20% is wasted effort until that's
  settled.

  Note on the brief: it still carries the memory-session header and a
  memory-specific checklist. I've reviewed the actual document and built a
  context-engineering checklist for §3.

  ---

  2. Scorecard

  Dimension: 1. Technical accuracy and currency
  Score /5: 3
  Justification: Nothing is wrong, but the load-bearing claims are unsourced —
  "about 80% of its capacity", the CPU/RAM analogy, the four failure modes, the
   "art and science" definition. Session 41 cites Chroma and Cognition for the
  same material; this doc cites nothing.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 2. Conceptual completeness
  Score /5: 3
  Justification: Strong on the problem and the four principles. Missing: any
  measurement, any cost numbers, and — for a session about managing context —
  anything on how you'd know it worked.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 3. Prerequisite calibration
  Score /5: 3
  Justification: Re-explains prompt engineering from scratch (Principle 2) to
  students who had Session 21. Ignores n8n, which they know and which fits this
   topic unusually well. Correctly pitches the agent material.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 4. Logical scaffolding
  Score /5: 4
  Justification: Genuinely good: challenge → why prompts aren't enough → three
  named problems → four principles → three advanced techniques → how to choose.
   Problem always precedes solution.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 5. Clarity and jargon control
  Score /5: 4
  Justification: Short sentences, concrete analogies (suitcase, CPU/RAM), no
  unexplained jargon. The best dimension.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Dimension: 6. Hands-on implementability
  Score /5: 1
  Justification: Zero Python. Zero workflow. Two fenced blocks total: a one-line

  SELECT and a sample NOTES.md. A student cannot build anything from this
  document.
  ────────────────────────────────────────

  nothing

  Right Approach".

  Score /5: 3
  Justification: Formatting is clean post-conversion. Attribution is the weak
  point: a visible third-party credit in one image and several borrowed
  formulations presented as the author's.

  Overall: 2.9 / 5

  4. Strengths

  1. "Choosing the Right Approach" (the five-row table). Task length →
     technique, from < 5 minutes to multi-session. This is the most useful thing
     in the document and I can't find an equivalent in Session 41. It converts
     four abstract techniques into a decision a student can actually make.
     Preserve exactly.
  2. Principle 3's good/bad tool triples. search_documents / find_files /
     query_database all overlapping, versus search_document_by_keyword /
     get_document_by_id / list_recent_documents. Concrete, immediately
     applicable, and it extends Session 19 rather than repeating it.
  3. The compaction before/after with token counts. 5,000 tokens of debugging
     history → a 500-token summary, with both versions shown. Numbers make the
     technique real in a way prose doesn't.
  4. The suitcase analogy (Principle 1). Holds up under pressure — "choose
     versatile items that can work in various situations" maps cleanly onto
     choosing diverse few-shot examples, which is exactly the point being made.
  5. The just-in-time retrieval example. "Load all 10,000 rows" versus one WHERE
     clause returning 50. One line of SQL does more teaching than a paragraph
     would.

  5. Findings

  #: 1
  Location: Whole document
  Issue: Substantial duplication of Session 41. I compared section by section.
  The opening bullets are near-verbatim ("Agents sometimes forget important
  information mid-conversation / Performance degrades as conversations get
  longer / Relevant information gets lost in long context windows / Costs
  increase with larger prompts"). "Traditional Approach vs Reality" is the
  same. The CPU/RAM analogy is the same. The four failure modes are the same
  four. CE-vs-PE is the same comparison. Minimal-high-quality-information is
  Session 41's "Quality Over Quantity". Compaction = 41's "Compressing
  Context"; note-taking = "Writing Context"; sub-agents = "Isolating Context";
  just-in-time retrieval = "Selecting Context". In several places 41 is 
  stronger — it cites Chroma's context-rot research and Cognition, and it gives
   fixes for each failure mode.
  Severity: Critical
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: If both ship, students sit through the same
  material twice and the second pass is thinner. If this replaces 41, the
  course loses the citations and the failure-mode fixes.
  Recommended fix: Decide the relationship first. Three viable options: (a) this

  becomes 41 — then port 41's citations, failure fixes and the OS→CE analogy
  line into it before replacing; (b) this becomes a second CE session that
  assumes 41 and covers only what's new — realistically the six-types map,
  agent strategies, CoT/few-shot, tool design, and "Choosing the Right
  Approach"; (c) merge into one longer session. My recommendation is (b):
  roughly 20% of this doc is genuinely new, and it's the more advanced 20%.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Location: Whole document
  Issue: No runnable code or workflow. Two fenced blocks exist: a one-line
  SELECT
  and a sample NOTES.md. Neither is executable as a lab. The brief states the
  purpose is "teach one sub-topic well enough that students can implement it in
   a hands-on lab."
  Severity: Critical
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: A student finishes able to describe compaction,
  note-taking and sub-agents, and unable to build any of them. Every other
  session in this course ends with working code.
  Recommended fix: Pick one technique and make it real. Compaction is the best
  candidate and needs ~20 lines: take a message list, count tokens, and when it
   crosses a threshold, call the model once to summarise everything except the
  last N messages, then continue. That single example makes "80% of capacity"
  concrete, gives the session a lab, and connects to the trim/delete/summarize
  strategies already taught in Session 31. UNVERIFIED — author to confirm which
   framework the lab should use; I'd suggest matching whatever Session 41's
  successor uses.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Location: Two <!-- GAP IN SOURCE --> markers
  Issue: Two sections have missing content. "An AI agent is a system that can" —

  the sentence stops mid-list. "Tip for effective prompting" — a heading with
  nothing under it.
  Severity: Critical
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Both sit exactly where a student expects the
  payoff. In a live session, the second is a slide the instructor cannot
  deliver.
  Recommended fix: Write the four or five agent capabilities; write the
  prompting
  tips. I deliberately did not invent either during conversion.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Location: "Context for AI Agents" and "Strategies and Tasks for Agents"
  Issue: Two entire sections are a heading plus an image, with zero prose. The
  six-types map has ~40 leaf nodes; the strategies image names eight
  strategies. None of that text exists in the document itself.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Anything the image doesn't render — a printout,
  a
  screen reader, a student reviewing on a phone — leaves these sections empty.
  And nothing is searchable or quotable.
  Recommended fix: Add a short prose list under each: the six context types as a

  one-line-each list, the eight strategies as a table with a one-line "when to
  use". The images then illustrate rather than carry.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Location: assets/ce-six-types-of-context.png
  Issue: Visible third-party credit — "productcompass.pm" is legible in the
  bottom-left of the rendered image. The other diagrams share a house style
  suggesting a common external source.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Not the student's problem, but a publishing risk

  for NxtWave.
  Recommended fix: Confirm licensing, or redraw. UNVERIFIED — author to confirm
  provenance of all seven; only image 4 carries a visible credit but the set
  looks related.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Location: Throughout
  Issue: Borrowed formulations presented without attribution. "The art and
  science of filling the LLM's context window with just the right information,
  in the right format, at the right time" is a widely circulated definition,
  not the author's. The CPU/RAM framing likewise. Session 41 links Chroma and
  quotes Cognition by name; this document cites nothing at all.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Students can't follow up, and it models bad
  citation practice.
  Recommended fix: Attribute the definition and the analogy, and carry over 41's

  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Location: "Technique 1: Compaction", step 1
  Issue: "When the context reaches about 80% of its capacity" — a specific
  operational threshold, unsourced.
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: It reads as a rule. A student will hardcode 0.8
  and assume it's established practice.
  Recommended fix: Either cite where 80% comes from, or reframe: "at some
  threshold — 70–80% is a common starting point — you summarise. The right
  number depends on how long your typical task runs; measure it."
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Location: Principle 2
  Issue: Re-teaches prompt engineering from scratch. "Prompt engineering is the
  practice of designing, refining, and optimizing inputs (prompts) given to
  Large Language Models…" to students who completed Session 21 (Effective
  Prompting Techniques).
  Severity: Major
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Over-explaining known material, in a document
  that under-explains the new material two sections later.
  Recommended fix: Cut the definition. Open Principle 2 with what's actually new

  — that a system prompt in an agent is reused every turn, so its token cost
  and its instructions compound in a way a one-off prompt's don't. Then keep
  the CoT/few-shot image, which is new (verified: Session 21 contains neither
  term).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 9
  Location: Whole document
  Issue: No n8n, though the audience knows it and this topic maps onto it more

  Severity: Major (pedagogy)
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: The one vocabulary they're already fluent in
  goes
  unused across a fully conceptual session.
  Recommended fix: One worked example: the sub-agent technique drawn as an n8n
  workflow — a main agent node delegating to two sub-workflows that each return
   a summary. It makes the most abstract technique concrete in the notation
  they know.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 10
  Location: Whole document

  Severity: Minor
  Impact on a 2nd-year student: Students learn four techniques and don't know

  6. Student-stall points

  Where: "An AI agent is a system that can"
  What stalls them: Hard stop. The sentence ends and the next thing is a
  heading.
  A student rereads it three times assuming they missed a page break.
  Fix: Finding 3.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: "Context for AI Agents" (image only)
  What stalls them: They hit a dense mind map with six branches and ~40 leaves,
  and no text telling them what to take from it. There's no way to know whether
   they're meant to memorise the six types or just notice the shape.
  Fix: Finding 4 — add the six types as a one-line-each list and say which
  matter
  most.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: "Strategies and Tasks for Agents" (image only)
  What stalls them: Same, worse — eight strategies with no indication of which
  are core and which are advanced.
  Fix: Finding 4.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: "Tip for effective prompting"
  What stalls them: A heading promising the practical payoff of Principle 2,
  followed by the next heading.
  Fix: Finding 3.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: "about 80% of its capacity"
  What stalls them: They will ask "80% of what — and how do I measure it?" The
  doc never explains how to count tokens or where the limit comes from.
  Fix: Finding 7, plus one clause on how you'd actually check (token count vs
  the
  model's context limit).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: "Compaction" vs "Compression" vs "Summarization"
  What stalls them: If this ships alongside Session 41, the same idea has two
  names across two sessions.
  Fix: Pick one term and use it in both.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: Principle 4's SQL
  What stalls them: SELECT * FROM sales WHERE quarter='Q3' assumes SQL
  familiarity that isn't in the prerequisite list.
  Fix: It's readable enough to survive, but one clause — "the tool runs a
  database query, filtered to just what was asked for" — makes it safe for a
  non-SQL reader.

  Terms used before definition: context window (used in the first challenge
  bullet, defined only later via the RAM analogy), tokens (first numeric use in
  "Information Overload", never defined), RAG (expanded on first use — good),
  sub-agents (used in the Choosing table before Technique 3 defines them).

  7. Rewrite examples

  A. The image-only section — "Context for AI Agents"

  Original

  ▎ Context for AI Agents
  ▎
  ▎ A mind map titled "6 Types of Context For AI Agents"… 
  ▎ (assets/ce-six-types-of-context.png)

  Revised

  ▎ Context for AI Agents
  ▎
  ▎ Everything an agent "knows" at any moment falls into six buckets. They 
  ▎ compete for the same limited window, which is why naming them matters:
  ▎
  ▎ | Type         | What it is                                          | 
  ▎ Typical size                |
  ▎ |--------------|-----------------------------------------------------|------
  ▎ -----------------------|
  ▎ | Instructions | Role, objective, rules, output format               | 
  ▎ Small, always present       |
  ▎ | Examples     | Worked demonstrations of what good looks like       | 
  ▎ Small, always present       |
  ▎ | Knowledge    | Domain facts, retrieved documents, task data        | 
  ▎ Large, varies per turn      |
  ▎ | Memory       | Short-term conversation state; long-term user facts | Grows
  ▎ over time             |
  ▎ | Tools        | Descriptions of what the agent can call             | 
  ▎ Fixed, often underestimated |
  ▎ | Tool results | What came back from those calls                     | Large
  ▎ and unpredictable     |
  ▎
  ▎ A mind map titled "6 Types of Context For AI Agents"… 
  ▎ (assets/ce-six-types-of-context.png)
  ▎
  ▎ Two of these surprise people. Tool descriptions are always in the window, 
  ▎ whether or not a tool is used — twenty tools is a permanent tax on every 
  ▎ turn. And tool results are the least predictable: one search can return more
  ▎ tokens than the entire conversation before it.

  What changed and why: the section now teaches without the image, so it
  survives print, screen readers and a phone. The table adds a "typical size"
  column, which is what makes the six types actionable in a session about a
  limited budget rather than a taxonomy to memorise. The closing two sentences
  are the practical insight the picture can't convey. The image stays, now
  illustrating rather than carrying.

  ---

  B. Principle 2's opening — over-explaining known material

  Original

  ▎ Principle 2: Smart System Prompts
  ▎
  ▎ Prompt engineering is the practice of designing, refining, and optimizing 
  ▎ inputs (prompts) given to Large Language Models (LLMs) to get your desired 
  ▎ output. The quality and effectiveness of LLMs are heavily influenced by the 
  ▎ prompts they receive…

  Revised

  ▎ Principle 2: Smart System Prompts
  ▎
  ▎ You already know how to write a good prompt. What changes in an agent is 
  ▎ that the system prompt is not written once and read once — it is re-sent on 
  ▎ every single turn.
  ▎
  ▎ That has two consequences worth designing around:
  ▎
  ▎ - Its tokens compound. A 500-token system prompt across a 40-turn 
  ▎ conversation is 20,000 tokens you paid for repeatedly. Tighten it once and 
  ▎ you save on every turn, forever.
  ▎ - Its instructions compete. Everything you add makes every other instruction
  ▎ slightly less salient. A system prompt with thirty rules follows them less 
  ▎ reliably than one with six.
  ▎
  ▎ So the goal is not the most complete system prompt. It is the smallest one 
  ▎ that still produces the behaviour you need.

  What changed and why: removes a definition Session 21 already gave and
  replaces it with the thing that is genuinely new — that in an agent the system
  prompt is a recurring cost and a crowded space. Same principle, same voice,
  but calibrated to what they know. Preference, not error: I'd keep the
  CoT/few-shot image immediately after, since that content is new to them.

  ---

  C. The unsourced threshold — Compaction step 1

  Original

  ▎ 1. When the context reaches about 80% of its capacity, the AI creates a 
  ▎ summary of the entire conversation.

  Revised

  ▎ 1. When the context gets close to full — somewhere around 70–80% is a common
  ▎ starting point — the AI summarises everything except the most recent 
  ▎ messages.

  ▎ How would you know? Count the tokens you are about to send and compare 
  ▎ against the model's context limit. That ratio is the trigger. The right 
  ▎ threshold depends on how long your typical task runs and how much room a 
  ▎ single tool result might suddenly need — start at 0.75, then adjust once you
  ▎ have seen it fire a few times.

  What changed and why: "about 80%" reads as an established constant; a student
  will hardcode it. Presenting it as a starting point they should tune, plus one
  sentence on how the check is actually made, turns an unsourced number into a
  measurable decision. Also fixes a small inaccuracy: the original says "summary
  of the entire conversation" in step 1 and "summary and the most recent
  messages" in step 2 — the revision makes step 1 consistent with step 2.

  ---

  8. Additions worth making

  1. The one worked example that gives this session a lab — a compaction loop.
  About 20 lines: hold a message list, count tokens before each call, and when
  the ratio crosses a threshold, make one model call to summarise everything but
  the last N messages and replace the history with [summary] + recent. Print
  the token count before and after. That single example makes the 80% threshold
  concrete, gives the session its missing hands-on path, and connects directly
  to trim/delete/summarize from Session 31. It is also the only technique in
  this doc that is genuinely buildable in a few minutes.

  2. An n8n rendering of the sub-agent technique. A main AI Agent node, two
  sub-workflow nodes (Web Research, Data Analysis), each returning a short
  summary to the coordinator. Roughly 120 words plus a screenshot. Sub-agents
  are the most abstract of the three techniques and n8n makes the token argument
  visible — the student can literally see that only the summaries come back on
  the main wire.

  3. A "what each failure looks like, and what to do" table — restoring what
  Session 41 has and this doc lost:

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │   Failure   │      What you'd notice      │     First thing to try      │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ The agent repeats a wrong   │ Validate before writing;    │
  │ Poisoning   │ fact confidently, across    │ don't persist unconfirmed   │
  │             │ turns                       │ facts                       │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Distraction │ It repeats earlier actions  │ Compact; drop stale tool    │
  │             │ instead of reasoning fresh  │ results                     │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Confusion   │ It calls the wrong tool     │ Reduce the tool list per    │
  │             │                             │ task                        │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Clash       │ It stalls or contradicts    │ Resolve conflicts on write, │
  │             │ itself                      │  don't append both          │
  └─────────────┴─────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  4. Five self-check questions:
  1. Your agent has 20 tools registered but a task needs 3. What does that cost
     you on every turn, even if none are called?
  2. A conversation is at 45,000 of a 50,000-token limit and the user asks a new
     question. Compaction or note-taking — and why?
  3. Which of the six context types is most likely to blow up unpredictably, and
     what would you do about it?
  4. You add a sub-agent to do web research. Where do the tokens go that the
     main agent never sees?
  5. Your agent keeps insisting a library has a function it doesn't. Which of
     the four failure modes is that, and what is your first move?

  5. One debugging exercise. Give students a conversation that has degraded and
  ask them to diagnose it from the transcript alone — is it distraction,
  confusion, or clash? Then apply the matching fix. This is the closest thing to
  a lab that a conceptual session can offer if code isn't added.
