  1. Failure modes — prose, recognition, and fixes

  The four were an image with no text. Rather than restate Session 41 (which
  already gives conceptual fixes), I wrote the practice half — how you spot each
  one in an agent you're actually running:

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │   Failure   │      What you actually see      │       Fix it with       │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │             │ States the same wrong thing     │                         │
  │ Poisoning   │ confidently, turn after turn,   │ Validate before writing │
  │             │ re-asserts it when corrected    │                         │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │             │ Repeats an action it already    │ Compaction — prune old  │
  │ Distraction │ did, re-runs a search it        │ tool output first       │
  │             │ already ran                     │                         │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │             │ Calls the wrong tool, or one    │ Cut the tool list;      │
  │ Confusion   │ that doesn't fit the question   │ sharpen descriptions    │
  │             │                                 │ (Principle 3)           │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Clash       │ Stalls, hedges, or flips        │ Resolve on write —      │
  │             │ between two answers             │ update, don't append    │
  └─────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  It opens by pointing back to Session 41 rather than re-teaching, and closes on
  the distinction that actually costs people time:

  ▎ Distraction is too much context; confusion is the wrong context. Compacting 
  ▎ a confused agent does nothing, and trimming tools from a distracted one does
  ▎ nothing either.

  That's the pairing most likely to be misdiagnosed, since both present as "the
  agent is being dumb."

  2. Vocabulary aligned

  The four techniques were already in the doc under different names, which would
  have silently broken the connection to Session 41. Added a "First, the Names"
  bridging table and tagged every heading:

  ┌────────────┬────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │ Session 41 │      In practice       │           Where            │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Write      │ Note-taking to a file  │ Technique 2                │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Select     │ Just-in-time retrieval │ Principle 4                │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Compress   │ Compaction             │ Technique 1 + the hands-on │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Isolate    │ Sub-agents             │ Technique 3                │
  └────────────┴────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  Plus the organising idea: select decides what comes in; write, compress and
  isolate all decide what goes out — to a file, a summary, or another agent.

  The "Choosing the Right Approach" table now carries the canonical names too,
  with one addition — tool-output pruning is deliberately excluded from it,
  because it isn't a per-task choice:

  ▎ It is close to free, so once your agent calls tools at all, leave it on.

  1. Both content gaps filled — from the course's own words, not invented

  The agent sentence now completes using Session 29's actual definition
  ("operate independently to achieve a goal without constant human
  intervention"), then adds the four capabilities and the line that earns the
  section its place here:

  ▎ Every one of those four steps either adds something to the context window or
  ▎ reads from it. That is why agents and context engineering are the same 
  ▎ subject: an agent is a program whose main job is deciding what goes into its
  ▎ own next prompt.

  "Tip for effective prompting" — I checked Session 21 first. It already teaches
  clarity, specificity, contextual awareness, output guidance and templates, so
  repeating them would have been the over-explaining problem I flagged earlier.
  Instead the section now references 21 and covers what's actually different: a
  system prompt is re-sent every turn, so its tokens compound and its rules
  compete. Then four agent-specific tips, including the one nobody does:

  ▎ Re-read it once a week. System prompts accumulate rules added to fix one-off
  ▎ failures, and those rules never get removed.

  The CoT/few-shot image now has prose explaining why each belongs in a context
  session — CoT for dense or contradictory retrieved documents, few-shot as
  Principle 1 in action.

  2. Provenance — fixed, not just flagged

  Rather than leave you a warning, I redrew the one image with the visible
  productcompass.pm credit. The replacement is better suited to the argument
  anyway: instead of a mind map, it shows all six types stacked inside one 
  window, sized by how much room they take, with the two that surprise people
  called out — tool descriptions as a standing tax, tool results as the culprit.
  It ends on:

  ▎ Space one type takes is space the others cannot have — that is the whole 
  ▎ problem.

  That also sets up the hands-on section directly. Source SVG is in assets/ so
  it's editable.

  The other six PNGs are still third-party. I can't verify their licensing, so
  assets/README.md now has a per-image provenance table marking exactly which is
  ours and which six need confirming.

  3. Both image-only sections now teach without the pictures

  "Context for AI Agents" and "Strategies and Tasks for Agents" were a heading
  plus an image and nothing else. Both now have tables. The strategies table
  bolds the four this session actually builds and leaves the other three as
  reference, so students know what to focus on.
